Moderate deviations for fully coupled multiscale weakly interacting particle systems Thanks: This work has been partially supported by the National Science Foundation (DMS 2107856) and Simons Foundation Award 672441. The authors of the paper would like to thank both reviewers for a very careful and constructive review of this article.
Z.W. Bezemek, and K. Spiliopoulos

Abstract
. We consider a collection of fully coupled weakly interacting diffusion processes moving in a two-scale environment. We study the moderate deviations principle of the empirical distribution of the particles’ positions in the combined limit as the number of particles grow to infinity and the time-scale separation parameter goes to zero simultaneously. We make use of weak convergence methods, which provide a convenient representation for the moderate deviations rate function in a variational form in terms of an effective mean field control problem. We rigorously obtain equivalent representation for the moderate deviations rate function in an appropriate “negative Sobolev” form, proving their equivalence, which is reminiscent of the large deviations rate function form for the empirical measure of weakly interacting diffusions obtained in the 1987 seminal paper by Dawson-Gärtner. In the course of the proof we obtain related ergodic theorems and we consider the regularity of Poisson type of equations associated to McKean-Vlasov problems, both of which are topics of independent interest. A novel “doubled corrector problem” is introduced in order to control derivatives in the measure arguments of the solutions to the related Poisson equations used to control behavior of fluctuation terms.

1.
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to study the moderate deviations principle (MDP) for slow-fast interacting particle systems. In particular, we consider the system

(1)

\displaystyle dX^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}

\displaystyle=\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon}b(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})+c(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})\biggr]dt+\sigma(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})dW^{i}_{t}

\displaystyle dY^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\epsilon}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon}f(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})+g(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})\biggr]dt

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{\epsilon}\biggl[\tau_{1}(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})dW^{i}_{t}+\tau_{2}(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t},\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t})dB^{i}_{t}\biggr]

\displaystyle(X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{0},Y^{i,\epsilon,N}_{0})

\displaystyle=(\eta^{x},\eta^{y}){}

on a filtered probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P},\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\})
with
\{\mathcal{F}_{t}\}
satisfying the usual conditions, where
b,c,\sigma,f,g,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
B^{i}_{t},W^{i}_{t}
are independent standard 1-D
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-Brownian motions for
i=1,...,N
, and
(\eta^{x},\eta^{y})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Here and throughout
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
denotes the space of probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
with finite second moment, equipped with the 2-Wasserstein metric (see Appendix
D
).
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
is defined by

(2)

\displaystyle\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}},t\in[0,T].

In (
1
),
X^{i,\epsilon,N}
and
Y^{i,\epsilon,N}
represent the slow and fast motion respectively of the
i^{\text{th}}
component. Note that classical models of interacting particles in a two-scale potential, see
[
7
,
11
,
51
,
61
]
, can be thought of as special cases of (
1
) with
Y^{i,\epsilon,N}=X^{i,\epsilon,N}/\epsilon
.

Assume that
\epsilon(N)\rightarrow 0
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. In our case, moderate deviations amounts to studying the behavior of the empirical measure of the particles, i.e., of
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
in the regime between fluctuations and large deviations behavior.
In particular, if we denote by
\mathcal{L}(X)
the process at which
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
converges to (the law of the averaged McKean-Vlasov Equation (
25
)) and consider the moderate deviation scaling sequence
\{a(N)\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
such that
a(N)>0,\forall N\in\mathbb{N}
with
a(N)\rightarrow 0
and
a(N)\sqrt{N}\rightarrow\infty
as
N\rightarrow\infty
,
the moderate deviations process is defined to be

(3)

\displaystyle Z^{N}_{t}\coloneqq a(N)\sqrt{N}(\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t}-\mathcal{L}(X_{t})),t\in[0,T].

The goal of this paper is to derive the large deviations principle with speed
a^{-2}(N)
for the process
Z^{N}_{t}
, which is the moderate deviations principle for the measure-valued process
\mu^{\epsilon,N}_{t}
. Notice that if
a(N)=1
then we get the standard fluctuations process whose limiting behavior amounts to fluctuations around the law of large numbers,
\mathcal{L}(X_{t})
, whereas if
a(N)=1/\sqrt{N}
then we would be in the large deviations regime.

We remark here that due to the effect of multiple scales, it turns out that a relation between
\epsilon
and
N
is needed. So beyond requiring
a(N)\rightarrow 0
and
a(N)\sqrt{N}\rightarrow\infty
, we also require that there exists
\rho\in(0,1)
and
\lambda\in(0,\infty]
such that
a(N)\sqrt{N}\epsilon(N)^{\rho}\rightarrow\lambda
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.
Note that this should be viewed as a restriction on the scaling sequence
a(N)
, not on the relationship between
\epsilon
and
N
, and in some regimes we expect this assumption can be weakened. See Remark
6.2
.

The presence of multiple scales is a common feature in a range of models used in various disciplines ranging from climate modeling to chemical physics to finance, see for example
[
58
,
52
,
63
,
70
,
50
,
35
]
for a representative, but by no means complete list of references. Interacting diffusions have also been the central topic of study in science and engineering, see for example
[
6
,
59
,
60
,
65
,
66
,
69
]
to name a few.
In the absence of multiple scales, i.e., when
\epsilon=1
, law of large numbers, fluctuations and large deviations behavior as
N\rightarrow\infty
has been studied in the literature, see
[
11
,
14
,
3
]
. Analogously, in the case of
N=1
, the behavior as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
, have been extensively studied in the literature, see for example
[
1
,
17
,
21
,
57
,
45
,
43
,
46
,
38
,
72
,
73
,
28
,
30
,
29
,
31
,
33
]
.

Homogenization of McKean-Vlasov equations (equations that are the limit of
N\rightarrow\infty
with
\epsilon
fixed) has also been recently studied in the literature, see e.g.,
[
5
,
54
,
67
,
74
]
. These results can be thought of as looking at the limit of the system (
1
) when first
N\rightarrow\infty
and then
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. Large deviations for a special case of (
1
) has been recently established in
[
7
]
and in the absence of multiple scales in
[
3
]
. In
[
25
]
the author studies large deviations for interacting particle systems in the absence of multiple scales but in the joint mean-field and small-noise limit. In the absence of multiple scales, i.e., when
\epsilon=1
, moderate deviations for interacting particle systems have been studied in
[
8
]
.

The contributions of this work are fourfold. Firstly, we investigate the combined limit
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
for the fully coupled interacting particle system of McKean-Vlasov type (
1
) through the lens of moderate deviations. In order to do so, we use the weak convergence methodology developed in
[
13
]
which leads to the study of (appropriately linearized) optimal stochastic control problems of McKean-Vlasov type, see for example
[
9
,
42
,
41
]
. The first main result of this paper is Theorem
3.2
which provides a variational representation of the moderate deviations rate function.

Secondly, we rigorously re-express the obtained variational form of the rate function in the “negative Sobolev” form given in Theorem 5.1 of the seminal paper by Dawson-Gärtner
[
14
]
in the absence of multiple scales. Hence, we rigorously establish the equivalence of the two formulations in the moderate deviations setting, see Proposition
4.3
. A connection of this form was recently established rigorously for the first time in the large deviations setting in
[
7
]
.

Thirdly, in the process of establishing the MDP, we derive related ergodic theorems for multiscale interacting particle systems that are of independent interest. Due to the nature of moderate deviations, we need to consider certain solutions of Poisson equations whose properties are considered for the first time in this paper. In particular, we must control a term involving a derivative in the measure argument of the solution to the Poisson Equation (
22
) (known as the Cell-Problem in the periodic setting). Such terms are unique to slow-fast interacting particle systems and slow-fast McKean-Vlasov SDEs, and thus do not appear whatsoever in proofs of averaging in the one-particle setting. Thus, the “doubled corrector problem” construction, (
63
), and the method of proof of Proposition
6.3
are novel ideas here, see also
[
5
]
.

Fourthly, in contrast to
[
8
]
, in this paper the coefficients of the model need not depend on the measure parameter in an affine way. We allow the coefficients of the interacting particle system (
1
) to have any dependence on the measure
\mu
, so long that it is sufficiently smooth- see Corollary
3.4
and Remark
3.5
. This is thanks to Lemma
D.7
, which is inspired by Lemma 5.10 in
[
15
]
, and allows us to see that with sufficient regularity of a functional on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the
L^{2}
error of that functional evaluated at the empirical measure of
N
IID random variables and the Law of those random variables is
\mathcal{O}(1/N)
as
N\rightarrow\infty
.

In Section
4.2
, we make our general results concrete for a popular model of interacting particles in a two-scale potential, see also
[
61
]
for a motivating example in this direction. In addition, we present in Section
C.3
a number of concrete examples where the conditions of this paper hold.

The identification of the optimal change of measure in the moderate deviations lower bound through feedback controls together with the equivalence proof between the variational formulation and the “negative Sobolev” form of the rate function, open the door to a rigorous study of provably-efficient accelerated Monte-Carlo schemes for rare events computation, analogous to what has been accomplished in the one particle case, see e.g.,
[
18
,
46
]
. Exploring this is beyond the scope of this work and will be addressed elsewhere.

In addition,
[
11
]
remarks that phase transitions can occur at the level of fluctuations for interacting particle systems. Since the moderate deviations principle is essentially a large deviations statement around the fluctuations, the results obtained in this paper can potentially be related to phase transitions and allow to characterize them further. This dynamical systems direction is left for future work as it is also outside the scope of this paper.

In contrast to large deviations, the main difficulty with moderate deviations lies in the tightness proof, where we use an appropriate coupling argument, as well as in the fact that the space of signed measures is not completely metrizable in the topology of weak convergence (see
[
12
]
Remark 1.2, as well as
[
27
]
Remarks 2.2 and 2.3 for further discussion on related issues). Thus, as we will see, we will have to study
Z^{N}
as a distribution-valued process on a suitable weighted Sobolev space. In addition, the presence of the multiple scales complicates the required estimates because the ergodic behavior needs to be accounted for as well. The coupling argument used in the proof of tightness is non-standard in that the IID particle system used as an intermediary process between the empirical measure
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
from Equation (
2
) and its homogenized McKean-Vlasov limit
\mathcal{L}(X)
from Equation (
25
) is not equal in distribution to
X
. Instead, it is an IID system of slow-fast McKean Vlasov SDEs - see Equation (
57
). Thus our proof of tightness is in a sense relying on the fact that the limits
N\rightarrow\infty
and
\epsilon\downarrow 0
for the empirical measure (
2
) commute at the level of the law of large numbers. For a further discussion of this, see Remark
5.1
and the discussion at the beginning of Section
7
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows. In Section
2
, we introduce the appropriate topology for
Z^{N}
and lay out our main assumptions. We also introduce a quite useful multi-index notation that will allow us to circumvent notational difficulties with various combinations of mixed derivatives that appear throughout the paper. The derivation of the moderate deviations principle is based on the weak convergence approach of
[
13
]
which converts the large deviations problem to weak convergence of an appropriate stochastic control problem. The main result is presented in Section
3
, Theorem
3.2
. In Section
4

we prove an alternative form of the rate function. This form provides a rigorous connection in moderate deviations between the “variational form” of the rate function for the empirical measure of weakly interacting particle systems proved in Theorem
3.2
to the “negative Sobolev” form given in Theorem 5.1 of the seminal paper by Dawson-Gärtner
[
14
]
. Corollaries
3.4
and
4.6
specialize the discussion to the setting without multiscale structure and thus generalizes the results of
[
8
]
. Specific examples are presented in Subsection
4.2
. Section
5
formulates the appropriate stochastic control problem.

Sections
6
-
10
are devoted to the proof of Theorem
3.2
. Due to the presence of the multiple scales, ergodic theorems are needed to characterize the behavior as
\epsilon\downarrow 0
of certain functionals of interest for the controlled multiscale interacting particle system; this is the content of Section
6
. Tightness of the controlled system is proven in Section
7
. In Section
8
we establish the limiting behavior of the controlled system. The Laplace principle lower bound is proven in Section
9
. Section
10
contains the proof of the Laplace principle upper bound as well as compactness of level sets. Conclusions and directions for future work are in Section
11
. Appendix
A
provides a list of technical notation used throughout the manuscript for convenience. A number of key technical estimates are presented in the remainder of the appendix. In particular, Appendix
B
contains moments bounds for the controlled system. Appendix
C
presents regularity results for the Poisson equation needed to study the fluctuations. Even though related results exist in the literature, the fully coupled McKean-Vlasov case is not covered by the existing results, and therefore Appendix
C
contains the appropriate discussion of the necessary extensions. Lastly, Appendix
D
contains necessary results on differentiation of functions on spaces of measures.

2.
Notation, Topologies, and Assumptions

In order to construct an appropriate topology for the process
Z^{N}
from Equation (
3
), we follow the method of
[
8
,
55
,
39
]
. Denote by
\mathcal{S}
the space of functions
\phi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which are infinitely differentiable and satisfy
|x|^{m}\phi^{(k)}(x)\rightarrow 0
as
|x|\rightarrow\infty
for all
m,k\in\mathbb{N}
. On
\mathcal{S}
, consider the sequence of inner products
(\cdot,\cdot)_{n}
and
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}_{n}
defined by

(4)

\displaystyle(\phi,\psi)_{n}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\int_{\mathbb{R}}(1+x^{2})^{2n}\phi^{(k)}(x)\psi^{(k)}(x)dx,\qquad\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{n}\coloneqq\sqrt{(\phi,\phi)_{n}}

for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
. As per
[
22
]
p.82 (this specific example on p.84), this sequence of seminorms induces a nuclear Fréchet topology on
\mathcal{S}
. Let
\mathcal{S}_{n}
be the completion of
\mathcal{S}
with respect to
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}_{n}
and
\mathcal{S}_{-n}=\mathcal{S}_{n}^{\prime}
the dual space of
\mathcal{S}_{n}
. We equip
\mathcal{S}_{-n}
with dual norm
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}_{-n}
and corresponding inner product
(\cdot,\cdot)_{-n}
. Then
\{\mathcal{S}_{n}\}_{n\in\mathbb{Z}}
defines a sequence of nested Hilbert spaces with
\mathcal{S}_{m}\subset\mathcal{S}_{n}
for
m\geq n
. In addition we have for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
m>n
such that the canonical embedding
\mathcal{S}_{-n}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-m}
is Hilbert-Schmidt. In particular, this holds for
m
sufficiently large that
\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi^{m}_{j}\right\rVert}_{n}<\infty
, where
\{\phi^{m}_{j}\}_{j\in\mathbb{N}}
is a complete orthonormal system of
\mathcal{S}_{m}
. This allows us to use the results of
[
36
]
to see that
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight as a sequence of
C([0,T];S_{-m})
-valued random variables for sufficiently large
m
.
In particular, we will require
m>7
to be sufficiently large so that the canonical embedding

(5)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-7}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-m}\text{ is Hilbert-Schmidt.}

In the proof of the Laplace Principle, we will also make use of
w>9
such that

(6)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-m-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-w}\text{ is Hilbert-Schmidt.}

When proving compactness of level sets of the rate function, we will in addition make use of
r>11
sufficiently large that the canonical embedding

(7)

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{-w-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-r}\text{ is Hilbert-Schmidt.}

It will be useful to consider another system of seminorms on
\mathcal{S}
given, for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, by

(8)

\displaystyle|\phi|_{n}\coloneqq\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}}|\phi^{(k)}(x)|{}

Via a standard Sobolev embedding argument, one can show that for each
n\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
C(n)
such that:

(9)

\displaystyle|\phi|_{n}\leq C(n)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{n+1},\forall\phi\in\mathcal{S}.

Let
\bm{X}
and
\bm{Y}
be Polish spaces, and
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\mu)
be a measure space. We will denote by
\mathcal{P}(\bm{X})
the space of probability measures on
\bm{X}
with the topology of weak convergence,
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\bm{X})\subset\mathcal{P}(\bm{X})
the space of square integrable probability measures on
\bm{X}
with the 2-Wasserstein metric (see Definition
D.1
),
\mathcal{B}(\bm{X})
the Borel
\sigma
-field of
\bm{X}
,
C(\bm{X};\bm{Y})
the space of continuous functions from
\bm{X}
to
\bm{Y}
,
C_{b}(\bm{X})
the space of bounded, continuous functions from
\bm{X}
to
\mathbb{R}
with norm
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}_{\infty}
, and
L^{p}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\mu)
the space of
p
-integrable functions on
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\mu)
(where if
\tilde{\Omega}=\bm{X}
and no
\sigma
-algebra is provided we assume it is
\mathcal{B}(\bm{X})
). For
\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\bm{X}),\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\bm{Y})
, we will denote the product measure induced by
\mu
and
\nu
on
\bm{X}\times\bm{Y}
by
\mu\otimes\nu
. We will at times denote
L^{2}(\bm{X}\times\bm{X},\mu\otimes\mu)
by
L^{2}(\bm{X},\mu)\otimes L^{2}(\bm{X},\mu)
. We will denote by
L^{1}_{\text{loc}}(\bm{X},\mu)
the space of locally integrable functions on
\bm{X}
. For
U\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
open, we will denote by
C^{\infty}_{c}(U)
the space of smooth, compactly supported functions on
U
.
C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
for
k\in\mathbb{N}
will note the space of functions with
k
continuous and bounded derivatives on
\mathbb{R}
, with norm
|\cdot|_{k}
as in Equation (
8
), and
C^{1,k}_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})
will denote continuous functions
\psi
on
[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}
with a continuous, bounded time derivative on
(0,T)
, denoted
\dot{\psi}
, such that
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\psi\right\rVert}_{C^{1,k}_{b}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R})}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R}}|\dot{\psi}(t,x)|+\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\psi(t,\cdot)\right\rVert}_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R})}<\infty
.
C^{k}_{b,L}(\mathbb{R})\subset C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
is the space of functions in
C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
such that all
k
derivatives are Lipschitz continuous. For
\phi\in L^{1}(\bm{X},\mu),\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R})
we define
\langle\mu,\phi\rangle\coloneqq\int_{\bm{X}}\phi(x)\mu(dx)
. Similarly, for
Z\in\mathcal{S}_{-p},\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{p}
, we will denote the action of
Z
on
\phi
by
\langle Z,\phi\rangle
. For
a,b\in\mathbb{R}
, we will denote
a\vee b=\max\{a,b\}
and
a\wedge b=\min\{a,b\}
.
C
will be used for a constant which may change from line to line throughout, and when there are parameters
a_{1},...,a_{n}
which
C
depends on in an important manner, will denote this dependence by
C(a_{1},...,a_{n})
. For all function spaces, the codomain is assumed to be
\mathbb{R}
unless otherwise denoted.

In the construction of the controlled system, we will also make use of the space of measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
such that
Q(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,t])=t,\forall t\in[0,T]
. We will denote this space
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We equip
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
with the topology of weak convergence of measures (thus making
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
a Polish space by
[
13
]
Theorem A.3.3). See also the proof of Lemma 3.3.1 in
[
13
]
for the fact that
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a closed subset of finite positive Borel measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d}\times[0,T]
). For when dealing with the occupation measures as defined in Equation (
56
), we will in particular take
d=4
and will interpret
Q(dx,dy,dz,dt)
as
x
denoting variable representing the first coordinate in
\mathbb{R}^{4}
,
y
the second, and
z
the third and fourth.

For a mapping
\vartheta\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,T]\rightarrow\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, it will be useful to define an element of
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
induced by
\vartheta
by

(10)

\displaystyle\nu_{\vartheta}(A\times[0,t])\coloneqq\int_{0}^{t}\vartheta(s)[A]ds,\forall t\in[0,T],A\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{d}).

Due to the nature of the space we consider the sequence
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
to live on, it is natural that we will have to restrict the growth of the coefficients which appear in Equations (
1
) and (
25
) in
x
. We will also need to ensure that the derivatives of the Poisson Equation which appear in the definition the limiting coefficients in Equation (
23
) exist and that the homogenized drift and diffusion coefficients in Equation (
24
), which determine the limiting McKean-Vlasov Equation
X_{t}
from Equation (
25
), are well-defined. In doing so, will be controlling many mixed derivatives of functions in the Lions sense
[
24
]
and in the standard sense, it will be useful for us to borrow the multi-index notation proposed in
[
44
]
and employed in
[
47
]
. For the reader’s convenience, we have included in Appendix
D
a brief review on differentiation of functions on spaces of measures. For a more comprehensive exposition on this, we refer the interested reader to
[
9
]
Chapter 5.

Furthermore, since we prove the moderate deviations principle via use of the controlled particle system (
55
), we will only have up to second moments of the controlled fast system (see Appendix
B
). It will be important to make sure that terms which the controlled fast process enters in the intermediate proofs of tightness, so naturally we will need some assumptions on the rate of polynomial growth in
y
of the coefficients which appear in Equations (
1
) and (
25
) (See Remark
2.7
). We thus extend the multi-index notation from the aforementioned papers to track specific collections of mixed partial derivatives, and to give us a clean way of tracking the rate of polynomial growth in
y
for those mixed partials in the coming definitions.

Definition 2.1
.

Let
n,l
be non-negative integers and
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
be an
n-
dimensional vector of non-negative integers. We call any ordered tuple of the form
(n,l,\bm{\beta})
a
multi-index
. For a function
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we will denote for a multi-index
(n,l,\bm{\beta})
, if this derivative is well defined,

\displaystyle D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]=\partial_{z_{1}}^{\beta_{1}}...\partial_{z_{n}}^{\beta_{n}}\partial_{x}^{l}\partial_{\mu}^{n}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}],

As noted in the Remark
D.3
, for such a derivative to be well defined we require for it to be jointly continuous in
x,\mu,z_{1},...,z_{n}
where the topology used in the measure component is that of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

We also define
\bm{\delta}^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]
in the exact same way, with the Lions derivatives
\partial_{\mu}
replaced by linear functional derivatives
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}
; see Appendix
D
for differentiation of functions on spaces of measures.

Definition 2.2
.

For
\bm{\zeta}
a collection of multi-indices of the form
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{n}
, we will call
\bm{\zeta}
a
complete
collection of multi-indices if for any
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
,
\{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(k))\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{k}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j\leq l,k\leq n,\bm{\alpha}(k)=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k}),\exists\bm{\beta}(k)=(\beta(k)_{1},...,\beta(k)_{k})\in\binom{\bm{\beta}}{k}\text{ such that }\alpha_{p}\leq\beta(k)_{p},\forall p=1,...,k\}\subset\bm{\zeta}
. Here for a vector of positive integers
\beta=(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
and
k\in\mathbb{N},k\leq n
, we are using the notation
\binom{\bm{\beta}}{k}
to represent the set of size
\binom{n}{k}
containing all the
k
-dimensional vectors of positive integers which can be obtained from removing
n-k
entries from
\bm{\beta}
.

Remark 2.3
.

Definition
2.2
is enforcing that if collection of multi-indices contains a multi-index representing some mixed derivative in
(x,\mu,z)
as per Definition
2.1
, then it also contains all lower-order mixed derivatives of the same type. For example, if
\bm{\zeta}
is a collection of multi-indices containing
(2,0,(1,1))
(corresponding to
\partial_{z_{1}}\partial_{z_{2}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]
) then, in order to be complete, it must also contain the terms
(2,0,(1,0)),(2,0,(0,1)),(2,0,0),(1,0,1),(1,0,0),
and
(0,0,0)
(corresponding to the terms
\partial_{z_{1}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]
,
\partial_{z_{2}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}],\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}],\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z]
,
\partial_{\mu}G(x,\mu)[z]
, and
G(x,\mu)
respectively). This is a technical requirement used in order to state the results in Appendix
C.1
in a way that allows the inductive arguments used therein to go through.

Using this multi-index notation, it will be useful to define some spaces regarding regularity of functions in regards to these mixed derivatives. We thus make the following modifications to Definition 2.13 in
[
47
]
:

Definition 2.4
.

For
\bm{\zeta}
a collection of multi-indices of the form
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{n}
, we define
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the class of functions
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]
exists and satisfies

(11)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert G\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\coloneqq\sup_{(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}}\sup_{x,z_{1},...,z_{n}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]|

\displaystyle\leq C.

We denote the class of functions
G\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
such that:

(12)

\displaystyle|D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]-D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})[z_{1}^{\prime},...,z_{n}^{\prime}]|

\displaystyle\leq C_{L}\biggl(|x-x^{\prime}|+\sum_{i=1}^{N}|z_{i}-z^{\prime}_{i}|+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})\biggr)

for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
and
x,x^{\prime},z_{1},...,z_{n},z_{1}^{\prime},...,z_{n}^{\prime}\in\mathbb{\mathbb{R}},\mu,\mu^{\prime}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
by
\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. We define
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
analogously, where instead here
\bm{\zeta}
is a collection of multi-indices of the form
(n,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{N}\times\mathbb{N}^{n}
, and we take the
l=0
in the above multi-index notation for the derivatives.

We will also make use of the class of functions
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
which contains
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
G(\cdot,y,\cdot)\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
for all
y\in\mathbb{R}
, with all derivatives appearing in the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
, and for each multi-index
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
,

(13)

\displaystyle\sup_{x,z_{1},...,z_{n}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]|

\displaystyle\leq C(1+|y|)^{q_{G}(n,l,\bm{\beta})},

where
q_{G}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{R}
.
Similarly,
\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
is defined as
G\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
such that Equation (
12
) holds for
G(\cdot,y,\cdot)
for each
y\in\mathbb{R}
, where
C_{L}(y)
grows at most polynomially in
y
.

We also define
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the class of functions
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
G(\cdot,x,\mu)
is continuously differentiable on
(0,T)
for all
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
with time derivative denoted by
\dot{G}(t,x,\mu)
,
G(t,\cdot,\cdot)\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
for all
t\in[0,T]
, with (
11
) holding uniformly in
t
, and
G,\dot{G},
and all derivatives involved in the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
are jointly continuous in time, measure, and space. We define for
G\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert G\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))}\coloneqq\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert G(t,\cdot)\right\rVert}_{\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))}+\sup_{t\in[0,T],x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\dot{G}(t,x,\mu)|.

We denote the class of functions
G\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
such that (
12
) holds uniformly in
t
by
\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. Again, we define
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, and
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
analogously.

At times we will want to consider Lions Derivatives bounded in
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)
rather than uniformly in
z
. Thus we define
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to be the class of functions
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]
exists and satisfies

(14)

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert G\right\rVert}_{\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\sup_{(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})^{\otimes n}}

\displaystyle=\sup_{(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}}\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}...\int_{\mathbb{R}}|D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]|^{2}\mu(dz_{1})...\mu(dz_{n})\biggr)^{1/2}\leq C.

We also define
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
analogously to
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
analogously to
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, and
\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
analogously to
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. We will denote the polynomial growth rate for
G\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
as in Equation (
13
) but with the
L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)^{\otimes n}
-norm by
\tilde{q}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathbb{R}
to avoid confusion with polynomial growth of the derivatives in the uniform norm. That is:

(15)

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\biggl(\int_{\mathbb{R}}...\int_{\mathbb{R}}|D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}G(x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]|^{2}\mu(dz_{1})...\mu(dz_{n})\biggr)^{1/2}\leq C(1+|y|)^{\tilde{q}_{G}(n,l,\bm{\beta})}.

Lastly, we define
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
in the same way as
\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
respectively, but with with
D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
replaced by
\bm{\delta}^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
. We also extend this in the natural way when the spatial components are in higher dimensions (i.e. taking gradients and using norms in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
).

Let us now introduce the main assumptions that are needed for the work of this paper to go through.

A1)

0<\lambda_{-}\leq\tau_{1}^{2}(x,y,\mu)+\tau_{2}^{2}(x,y,\mu)\leq\lambda_{+}<\infty
,
\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, and
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
have two uniformly bounded derivatives in
y
and which are jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2}).

A2)

There exists
\beta>0
and
\kappa>0
such that:

(16)

\displaystyle f(x,y,\mu)=-\kappa y+\eta(x,y,\mu)

where
\eta
is uniformly bounded in
x
and
\mu
, and Lipschitz in the sense of
A9)
in
x
,
\mu
, and
y
with

\displaystyle|\eta(x,y_{1},\mu)-\eta(x,y_{2},\mu)|\leq L_{\eta}|y_{1}-y_{2}|,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

for
L_{\eta}
such that
L_{\eta}-\kappa<0
, and

(17)

\displaystyle 2(f(x,y_{1},\mu)-f(x,y_{2},\mu))(y_{1}-y_{2})+3|\tau_{1}(x,y_{1},\mu)-\tau_{1}(x,y_{2},\mu)|^{2}

\displaystyle+3|\tau_{2}(x,y_{1},\mu)-\tau_{2}(x,y_{2},\mu)|^{2}\leq-\beta|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{2},

\displaystyle\forall x,y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}).

Let
a(x,y,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}[\tau_{1}^{2}(x,y,\mu)+\tau_{2}^{2}(x,y,\mu)]
. For
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, we define the differential operator
L_{x,\mu}
acting on
\phi\in C_{b}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
by

(18)

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}\phi(y)=f(x,y,\mu)\phi^{\prime}(y)+a(x,y,\mu)\phi^{\prime\prime}(y).

Note that under assumptions
A1)
and
A2)
, there is a constant
C
independent of
x,y,\mu
such that:

(19)

\displaystyle 2f(x,y,\mu)y+3|\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)|^{2}+3|\tau_{2}(x,y,\mu)|^{2}\leq-\frac{\beta}{2}|y|^{2}+C,\forall x,y\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Thus by
[
72
]
Proposition 1 (see also
[
32
]
), there exists a
\pi(\cdot;x,\mu)
which is the unique measure solving

(20)

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}^{*}\pi=0.

Moreover, for all
k>0
, there is
C_{k}\geq 0
such that
\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}|y|^{k}\pi(dy;x,\mu)\leq C_{k}
.

A3)

For
\pi
as in Equation (
20
),

(21)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}b(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}),

b
is jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
, grows at most polynomially in
y
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Having introduced the notation above, we can now present the law of large numbers for the empirical measure
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
from Equation
2
in the joint limit as
\epsilon\downarrow 0,N\rightarrow\infty
. Under assumptions
A1)
-
A3)
, by Lemma
C.1
we consider
\Phi
the unique classical solution to:

(22)

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}\Phi(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=-b(x,y,\mu),\qquad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0.{}

Let us define the functions

(23)

\displaystyle\gamma(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\gamma_{1}(x,y,\mu)+c(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle\gamma_{1}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq b(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{x}(x,y,\mu)+g(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)+\sigma(x,y,\mu)\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{xy}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle D(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq D_{1}(x,y,\mu)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle D_{1}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=b(x,y,\mu)\Phi(x,y,\mu)+\sigma(x,y,\mu)\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu).

and

(24)

\displaystyle\bar{\gamma}(x,\mu)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\gamma(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\biggr],\qquad\bar{D}(x,\mu)\coloneqq\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}D(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\biggr].

Then, by essentially the same arguments as in
[
7
]
, under the conditions outlined below,
\mu^{\epsilon,N}
converges in distribution to the deterministic limit
\mathcal{L}(X)
where
X
satisfies the averaged McKean-Vlasov SDE

(25)

\displaystyle dX_{t}

\displaystyle=\bar{\gamma}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))dt+\sqrt{2\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))}dW^{2}_{t}\quad X_{0}=\eta^{x}.

Here
W^{2}_{t}
is a Brownian motion on another filtered probability space satisfying the usual conditions.
In fact, we see here in Lemma
8.2
that in fact this convergence occurs in
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for each
t\in[0,T].

Remark 2.5
.

Using an integration-by-parts argument, one can find that the diffusion coefficient
\bar{D}
can be written in the alternative form

(26)

\displaystyle\bar{D}(x,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left([\tau_{2}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)]^{2}+[\sigma(x,y,\mu)+\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)]^{2}\right)\pi(dy;x,\mu),

and hence is non-negative. See
[
4
]
Chapter 3 Section 6.2 for a similar computation.

We now introduce the remaining assumptions. Since we are dealing with fluctuations, we will need to be able to obtain rates of averaging, and thus there are several auxiliary Poisson equations involved in the proof of tightness. When there is more specific structure to the system of equations (
1
), these assumptions may be able to be verified on a case-by-case basis. In Subsection
C.3
we provide concrete examples for which all of the conditions imposed in the paper hold. Remark
2.6
and mainly Remark
2.7
discuss the meaning of these assumptions more thoroughly.
In doing so, it will be useful to define the following complete collections of multi-indices in the sense of Definitions
2.1
and
2.2
:

(27)

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j_{1},0),(1,j_{2},j_{3}),(2,j_{4},(j_{5},j_{6})),(3,0,(j_{7},0,0))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}\in\{0,1,...4\},j_{2}+j_{3}\leq 4,j_{4}+j_{5}+j_{6}\leq 2,j_{7}=0,1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j_{1},0),(1,j_{2},j_{3}),(2,0,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}=0,1,2,j_{2}+j_{3}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}

\displaystyle\ni\{(j_{1},j_{2},0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}+j_{2}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{2}

\displaystyle\ni\{(j,0,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j=0,1\}

\displaystyle\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{3}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j_{1},0),(1,0,j_{2})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}=0,1,2,j_{2}=0,1\}

\displaystyle\bm{\zeta}_{x,l}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j=0,1,..,l\},l\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{\zeta}}

\displaystyle\ni\{(j,0,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j=0,1,2\}

\displaystyle\bar{\bm{\zeta}}_{l}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,0,0),(1,0,j)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j=0,1,...,l\},l\in\mathbb{N}.

In the following set of assumptions, recall that for
G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
and a multi-index
(n,l,\beta)
,
\tilde{q}_{G}(n,l,\beta)
denotes the rate of polynomial growth in
y
of the mixed derivative of
G
corresponding to
(n,l,\beta)
as per Equation (
15
) in Definition
2.4
. Recall also the spaces of functions of measures from Definition
2.4
.

A4)

Strong existence and uniqueness holds for the system of SDEs (
1
) for all
N\in\mathbb{N}
, for the Slow-Fast McKean-Vlasov SDEs (
57
), and for the limiting McKean-Vlasov SDE (
25
).

A5)

g
and
\sigma
are uniformly bounded, and
c,b
grow at most linearly in
y
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. All coefficients are jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2}).

A6)

There exists a unique strong solution
\Phi\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to Equation (
22
) with
\tilde{q}_{\Phi}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}
, and
\Phi_{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, with
\tilde{q}_{\Phi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}_{2}}
. In addition, this can be strengthened to
\tilde{q}_{\Phi}(0,k,0)\leq 0,k=0,1
and
\tilde{q}_{\Phi_{y}}(0,0,0)\leq 0
. (For Proposition
6.1
and Theorem
7.2
).

A7)

There exists a unique strong solution
\chi\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to Equation (
63
) with
\tilde{q}_{\chi}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}
, and
\chi_{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
\chi_{yy}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{(0,0,0)}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
with
\tilde{q}_{\chi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}_{1}}
,
\tilde{q}_{\chi_{yy}}(0,0,0)\leq 1
. In addition, this can be strengthened to
\tilde{q}_{\chi}(0,k,0)\leq 0,k=0,1
and
\tilde{q}_{\chi_{y}}(0,0,0)\leq 0
. (For Proposition
6.3
and Theorem
7.2
).

A8)

For
F=\gamma,D,
or
\sigma\psi_{1}+[\tau_{1}\psi_{1}+\tau_{2}\psi_{2}]\Phi_{y}
for any
\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
, there exists a unique strong solution
\Xi\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to Equation (
64
) with each of these choices of
F
,
\tilde{q}_{\Xi}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}
, and
\Xi_{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
with
\tilde{q}_{\Xi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
. Moreover, we assume for all choices of
F
, this can be strengthened to
\tilde{q}_{\Xi}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
and
\tilde{q}_{\Xi_{y}}(0,0,0)\leq 1
. (For Propositions
6.4
/
10.1
and Theorem
7.2
).

A9)

For
F=\gamma,\sigma+\tau_{1}\Phi_{y},\tau_{2}\Phi_{y},\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
:

\displaystyle|F(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-F(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})|\leq C(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})),\forall x_{1},x_{2},y\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(Lemmas
7.4
and
7.5
).

A10)

\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. (For Theorem
7.2
).

A11)

Consider the Poisson equation

(28)

\displaystyle L^{2}_{x,\bar{x},\mu}\tilde{\chi}(x,\bar{x},y,\bar{y},\mu)

\displaystyle=-b(x,y,\mu)\Phi(\bar{x},\bar{y},\mu),\quad\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\tilde{\chi}(x,\bar{x},y,\bar{y},\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\pi(d\bar{y},\bar{x},\mu)=0.

where
L^{2}_{x,\bar{x},\mu}
is as in Equation (
60
). Assume there exists a unique strong solution
\tilde{\chi}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{3}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and
\tilde{\chi_{y}}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,1}}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
to Equation (
28
). (For Theorem
7.2
).

A12)

\tau_{1},\tau_{2},f,\gamma,\sigma+\tau_{1}\Phi_{y},\tau_{2}\Phi_{y}\in\mathcal{M}^{\bar{\bm{\zeta}}}_{\bm{\delta},p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})).
(For Lemmas
7.4
and
7.5
).

A13)

For
w
as in Equation (
6
) and
\bar{\gamma}
,
\bar{D}
as in Equation (
24
),
\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bar{\bm{\zeta}}_{w+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, and

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\bar{\gamma}(x,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2}+\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\bar{D}(x,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2}<\infty.

(For Lemmas

,

,

and Proposition

).

Remark 2.6
.

There is a current gap in the literature regarding rates of polynomial growth for derivatives of solutions to Poisson Equations of the form (
22
), as outlined in
[
68
]
Remark A.1. Though in Proposition A.2 they state a result partially amending this issue, the bounds provided likely are not tight. In particular, under the assumption
A2)
which we require for moment bounds of the fast process (and hence slow) process in Section
B
, their result cannot provide boundedness of derivatives in
y
of
\Phi
from (
22
), or any of the other auxiliary Poisson equations which we consider. This in turn also makes it difficult to gain good rates of polynomial growth for derivatives in the parameters
x
and
\mu
. We need strict control of these rates of growth, for the reasons outlined in Remark
2.7
. Stronger bounds are derived in the 1-D case in Proposition A.4 of
[
68
]
, so this makes gaining the necessary control much easier in the current setting (see the results contained in Subsection
C.1
in the Appendix). Note also the much stricter assumptions imposed when handling the multi-dimensional cell problem in Lemma
C.6
(which is required to establish sufficient conditions for
A7)
).

Remark 2.7
.

Assumptions
A1)
and
A2)
are used in tandem for the existence and uniqueness of the invariant measure
\pi
from Equation (
20
). Such an invariant measure exists under weaker recurrence conditions on
f
(see, e.g.
[
72
]
Proposition 1 ), but we use the near-Ornstein–Uhlenbeck structure assumed in (
16
) and the form of the retraction to the mean (
17
) in order to prove certain moment bounds on the controlled fast process in the Appendix
B
, and (
17
) is also used in order to gain sufficient conditions for the required regularity of the Poisson Equations in Assumptions
A6)
-
A13)
in Appendix
C
. In particular, (
16
) is inspired by Assumption 4.1 (iii) in
[
45
]
and is needed for Lemma
B.2
, and (
17
) is a standard assumption for control of moments of SDEs over infinite time horizons and for controlling solutions of related Cauchy problems (see e.g.
[
74
]
Assumption A.1 Equation (2.3)).

The centering condition
A3)
is standard in the theory of stochastic homogenization. Assumption
A4)
is required in order to apply the weak-convergence approach to large deviations. In particular, it ensures that the prelimit control representation (
51
) holds. This is known to hold, for example, under global Lipschitz assumptions on all the coefficients (see, e.g.
[
34
]
Theorem 2.1 and Section 6.1 in
[
74
]
), though can also be proved under much weaker assumptions. These two assumptions, along with existence and uniqueness of the invariant measure
\pi
from Equation (
20
) and the Poisson Equation
\Phi
from Equation (
22
), can be seen as the crucial hypothesis of this paper. The rest of the assumptions are technical and essentially used to have sufficient conditions for tightness of the controlled fluctuations processes
\tilde{Z}^{N}
from Equation ((
54
)) (and, in the case of Assumption
A13)
, to have uniqueness of solutions to its limit (
32
)).

The boundedness and linear growth of the coefficients from Assumption
A5)
are used to restrict the growth of the coefficients so that second moments of the controlled fast process
\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}
from Equation (
55
) can be proved in Appendix
B
, and to ensure that only knowing these second moment bounds are sufficient for boundedness of the remainder terms in e.g. the ergodic-type theorems of Section
6
. The joint continuity assumption is used to ensure that integrating the coefficients is a continuous function on the space of measures.

The Assumptions
A6)
-
A13)
are listed in terms of the Poisson Equations and averaged coefficients (and hence implicitly in terms of
\Phi
from Assumption
A6)
) because these assumptions can be verified on a case-by-case basis when the differential operator (
18
) or the inhomogeneities considered have some special structure. See the Examples provided in Appendix
C.3
.

The growth required by the specific derivatives listed in Assumptions
A6)
-
A8)
are imposed in order to ensure that the remainder terms resulting form Itô’s formula in the Ergodic-Type Theorems in Section
6

are bounded. In particular, in Section
6
, we are dealing with the controlled slow-fast system (
55
), which due to the controls a priori being at best
L^{2}
integrable (see the bound (
53
)), we are only able to show that we have 2 bounded moments of the fast component (see Appendix
B
). This is limiting, since the terms which show up in the Ergodic-Type Theorems are products of derivatives of the Poisson equation with the coefficients of the system (
1
), of which
c
and
b
may grow linearly as per assumption
A5)
, and with the
L^{2}
controls.

Using Assumption
A6)
as an example and unpacking the multi-index notation, we are requiring
\Phi,\Phi_{x},\Phi_{y}
are bounded, and
\Phi_{xx},\partial_{\mu}\Phi,\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{x},\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{y},\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\Phi,\partial^{2}_{\mu}\Phi
grow at most linearly in
y
in their appropriate norms. Looking at the proof of Proposition
6.1
, since we are taking the
L^{2}
norm of the remainder terms
\tilde{B}_{1}-\tilde{B}_{8}
, we are essentially ensuring all the products showing up in these terms are
L^{2}
bounded. In particular, in
\tilde{B}_{7}
, the controls are multiplied by
\Phi
and
\Phi_{x}
, which is why we end up needing those derivatives to be bounded.
\Phi_{y}
being bounded is needed elsewhere for essentially the same reason - see, e.g. the proof of Proposition
10.2
, where we use that
B^{N}_{t}
is bounded in
L^{2}
. The reasoning behind the Assumptions
A7)
and
A8)
are the exact same, with additional regularity of
\chi_{y}
and
\Xi_{y}
(replacing
\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{2}
by
\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
means we are requiring
\chi_{y}
and
\Xi_{y}
have an
x
derivative which grows at most linearly in addition to a
\mu
derivative) and
\chi_{yy}
required due to those additional terms showing up in
\bar{B}_{2}
in Proposition
6.3
,
C_{2}
in Proposition
6.4
, and
\bar{B}_{13}
in Proposition
6.3
respectively.

The Lipschitz continuity imposed in Assumption
A9)
and the existence of two linear functional derivatives which grow at most polynomially in
y
uniformly in
x,\mu
imposed in Assumption
A12)
are used to couple the controlled particles (
55
) to the auxiliary IID particles (
57
) in Subsection
7.2
. In particular, the terms required to be Lipschitz are those which show up in the drift and diffusion of the processes which result from applying Proposition
6.1

to the controlled system and IID system respectively. The use of a Lipschitz property in such a coupling argument is standard - see, e.g. Lemma 1 in
[
55
]
. Since we don’t assume that the coefficients have linear interaction with the measure, Assumption
A12)
is being used to apply Lemma
D.7
to the listed functions. The result of that Lemma is essentially the Assumption (S3) made in
[
39
]
, which we are using in essentially the same manner that they are in their coupling argument in Theorem 2.4.

Assumption
A10)
is tailored to ensure enough regularity of the coefficients of the Cauchy Problem on Wasserstein Space for Theorem
7.2
to hold- see
[
5
]
(in particular Lemma 5.1 therein). Assumption
A11)
is used to apply the same result, and requires the introduction of the additional auxiliary Poisson equation
A11)
which is defined similarly to
\chi
from Assumption
A7)
but with a different inhomogeneity due to an additional term which arises in
[
5
]
Proposition 4.4 due to the McKean-Vlasov dynamics. The use of this specific control over the derivatives of
\tilde{\chi}
is discussed after the statement of Theorem
7.2
.

Finally, Assumption
A13)
is needed for well-definedness/uniqueness of the limiting Equation (
32
). See the analogous Assumptions 2.2/2.3 in
[
8
]
.

3.
Main Results

We are now ready to state our main result, which takes the form of Theorem
3.2
below. These results will be applied to a concrete class of examples of interacting particle systems of the form (
1
) in Subsection
4.2
.

We prove the large deviations principle for fluctuations process
\{Z^{N}\}
from Equation (
3
) via means of the Laplace Principle. In other words, in Theorem
3.2
, we identify the rate function
I\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
such that for
w
as in Equation (
6
):

(29)

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}-a^{2}(N)\log\mathbb{E}\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)}F(Z^{N})\biggr)=\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];S_{-w})}\{I(Z)+F(Z)\}

for all
F\in C_{b}(C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\tau}))
, for any
\tau\geq w
. In particular, this holds for all
F\in C_{b}(C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r}))
for
r>w+2
as in Equation (
7
), and for such
F
the right hand side is equal to
\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];S_{-r})}\{I(Z)+F(Z)\}
by construction of
I
(see Theorem
3.2
). The equality (
29
) along with compactness of level sets of
I
implies that
\{Z^{N}\}
satisfies the large deviations principle with speed
a^{-2}(N)
and rate function
I
via e.g. Theorem 1.2.3 in
[
13
]
.

In order to show (
29
), we will show in Section
9
that the Laplace principle Lower Bound:

(30)

\displaystyle\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}-a^{2}(N)\log\mathbb{E}\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)}F(Z^{N})\biggr)\geq\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];S_{-w})}\{I(Z)+F(Z)\},\forall F\in C_{b}(C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\tau}))

for any
\tau\geq w
, with
w
as in Equation (
6
), holds.

Then, in Section
10
we will prove the Laplace principle Upper Bound:

(31)

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}-a^{2}(N)\log\mathbb{E}\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)}F(Z^{N})\biggr)\leq\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];S_{-w})}\{I(Z)+F(Z)\},\forall F\in C_{b}(C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\tau}))

for any
\tau\geq w
holds and compactness of level sets of
I
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
, at which point the moderate deviations principle of Theorem
3.2
will be established.

We now formulate the rate function. Consider the controlled limiting equation:

(32)

\displaystyle\langle Z_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi(\cdot)\rangle ds+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)Q(dx,dy,dz,ds)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(x)Q(dx,dy,dz,ds)

\displaystyle\bar{L}_{\nu}\phi(x)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bar{\gamma}(x,\nu)\phi^{\prime}(x)+\bar{D}(x,\nu)\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\bar{\gamma}(z,\nu)[x]\phi^{\prime}(z)+\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\bar{D}(z,\nu)[x]\phi^{\prime\prime}(z)\right)\nu(dz),\nu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}).

for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
. Here we recall the limiting coefficients
\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}
from Equation (
24
), the limiting McKean-Vlasov Equation
X_{t}
from Equation (
25
), and the linear functional derivative
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}
from Definition
D.4
.

.

Let assumptions

-

hold. Then
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies the Laplace principle (

) with rate function

given by

(33)

\displaystyle I(Z)=\inf_{Q\in P^{*}(Z)}\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q(dx,dy,dz,ds)\biggr\}{}

where
Q\in M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
(recall this space from above Equation (

)) is in
P^{*}(Z)
if:

(
P^{enumi}

(Z,Q)
satisfies Equation (

)

(

(

Disintegrating

,
\lambda(dy;x,s)=\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))

\nu_{\mathcal{L}(X_{\cdot})}

Here we use the convention that
\inf\{\emptyset\}=+\infty
.

Replacing assumption
A13)
by the following:

A’13)

For

as in Equation (

) and
\bar{\gamma}
,

as in Equation (

\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,r+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bar{\bm{\zeta}}_{r+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))

we can in addition prove compactness of level sets of the rate function given in (

Theorem 3.2
.

, and also impose that

for

.

As is typically the case when using the weak convergence approach of
[

]
to prove a large deviations principle, the rate function (

) can also be characterized by controls in feedback form:

Corollary 3.3
.

In the setting of Theorem
3.1
, we can alternatively characterize the rate function as:

(34)

\displaystyle I^{o}(Z)=\inf_{h\in P^{o}(Z)}\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds\biggr\}

where
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}

if:

(
P^{o}

(Z,h)
satisfies Equation (

) for all

and

(

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds<\infty.

Here we define:

(35)

\displaystyle\langle Z_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi(\cdot)\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\left[\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\times\right.

\displaystyle\hskip 199.16928pt\left.\times\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\right.\biggr]ds

Again, we use the convention that
\inf\{\emptyset\}=+\infty
. In the setting of Theorem
3.2
, we also impose that
I^{o}(Z)=+\infty
for
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})\setminus C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
.

Proof.

This follows from Jensen’s inequality and the affine dependence of the coefficients on the controls. The details are omitted for brevity given that the argument is standard, e.g., see Section 5 in
[
17
]
.
∎

In addition, as a corollary to the proof of Theorem
3.2
, we extend the results from
[
8
]
as follows:

Corollary 3.4
.

(MDP without Multiscale Structure)
Suppose that
b=f=g=\tau_{1}=\tau_{2}\equiv 0
and
c(x,y,\mu)=c(x,\mu),\sigma(x,y,\mu)=\sigma(x,\mu)
. Let
v>4
be sufficiently large that the canonical embedding
\mathcal{S}_{-4}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-v}
is Hilbert-Schmidt,
\rho>6
be sufficiently large that the canonical embedding
\mathcal{S}_{-v-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-\rho}
is Hilbert-Schmidt, and
\bar{\bm{\zeta}}
as in (
27
). Assume also that
\sigma,c\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and for
F(x,\mu)=c(x,\mu)
or
\sigma(x,\mu)
:

(1)

\sup_{\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|F(\cdot,\mu)|_{\rho+2}<\infty

(2)

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}F(x,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{\rho+2}<\infty
.

Here we recall the space
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}
from Definition
2.4
, the collection of multi-indices
\bm{\zeta}
from Equation (
27
), and the norms on
\mathcal{S}
defined in Equations (
4
) and (
8
).
Then
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviation principle on the space
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})
with speed
a^{-2}(N)
and good rate function
\tilde{I}^{o}
given by

(36)

\displaystyle\tilde{I}^{o}(Z)=\inf_{h\in\tilde{P}^{o}(Z)}\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|h(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\biggr\}

where
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is in
\tilde{P}^{o}(Z)
if:

(
P^{o}
1)

(Z,h)
satisfies Equation (
37
) for all
t\in[0,T]
and
\phi\in C_{c}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})

(
P^{o}
2)

\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|h(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds<\infty

and
\inf\{\emptyset\}=+\infty
,
I(Z)=+\infty
for
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})\setminus C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-v})
.
Here we define:

(37)

\displaystyle\langle Z_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\tilde{L}_{\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}_{s})}\phi(\cdot)\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sigma(\tilde{X}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}_{s}))h(s,\tilde{X}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}_{s})\biggr]ds

\displaystyle\tilde{L}_{\nu}\phi(x)

\displaystyle=c(x,\nu)\phi^{\prime}(x)+\frac{\sigma^{2}(x,\nu)}{2}\phi^{\prime\prime}(x)+\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(\frac{\delta}{\delta m}c(z,\nu)[x]\phi^{\prime}(z)+\frac{1}{2}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}[\sigma^{2}(z,\nu)[x]]\phi^{\prime\prime}(z)\right)\nu(dz)

\displaystyle\tilde{X}_{t}

\displaystyle=\eta^{x}+\int_{0}^{t}c(\tilde{X}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}_{s}))ds+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(\tilde{X}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}_{s}))dW_{s}.

Proof.

This follows from Theorem
3.2
. The assumptions needed are vastly simplified due to the absence of multiscale structure. In particular, we have no need for the results from Section
6
and Subsection
7.1
. The rate function can be posed on a smaller space
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})
(agreeing with that of Theorem 2.1 in
[
8
]
) as opposed to the larger
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
of our Theorem
3.2
due to the IID system (
57
) not depending on
\epsilon
in this regime. In particular, this means
\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}\overset{d}{=}\tilde{X}_{t}
in the proof of Lemma
7.6
, and hence the result is improved
C(T)|\phi|^{2}_{1}
instead of
C(T)|\phi|^{2}_{4}
. Similarly, in the result of Lemma
7.7
, the bound on
R^{N}_{t}(\phi)
can be improved from
\bar{R}(N,T)|\phi|_{4}
to
\bar{R}(N,T)|\phi|_{3}
using Lemma
7.5
and the proof method of Proposition 4.2 in
[
8
]
. At this point tightness of
\{\tilde{Z}^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
from Equation (
54
) can be proved in Proposition
7.8
, but with the uniform 7-continuity of Equation (
67
) improved to uniform 4-continuity, and hence the result holds with
w
replaced by
v
. The remainder of the proofs in the paper found in Subsection
7.4
and Sections
8
, and
9
then go through verbatim with
m
and
w
replaced by
v
and
r
replaced with
\rho
, but with the simplifications assumed on the coefficients allowing us to set many terms equal to
0
. In particular, in the controlled particle Equation (
55
), we can set
\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}\equiv 0
, and throughout the invariant measure
\pi
from Equation (
20
) can be set to
\delta_{0}
, which makes dealing with the second marginals of the occupation measures
\{Q^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
from Equation (
56
) trivial. Lastly, in Section
10
, due to the lack of multiscale structure, there is no need for an approximation argument in the proof of Proposition
10.1
, and hence existence of solutions to (
37
) can be established in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-v})
and compactness of level sets established in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})
exactly as in Subsections 4.4 and 4.5 of
[
8
]
.
∎

Remark 3.5
.

Note that, in contrast to
[
8
]
, which assumes a linear-in-measure form of the coefficients of Equation (
1
) (without multiscale structure), i.e. that there are
\beta,\alpha\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{2}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
c(x,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\beta(x,z)\mu(dz),\sigma(x,\mu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\alpha(x,z)\mu(dz)
, we do not suppose any particular form of
c(x,\mu)
,
\sigma(x,\mu)
other than that they have sufficient regularity for the proof of tightness and existence/uniqueness of the limiting equation. We are able to do so via the use of Lemma
D.7
(which holds also in the case without dependence of the function
p
on
y
) and the assumption that
\sigma,c\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. For the specific linear form of
c
and
\sigma
assumed by
[
8
]
,
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}c(x,\mu)[z]=\beta(x,z)
and
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\sigma(x,\mu)[z]=\alpha(x,z)
, so the condition (2) from Corollary
3.4
in fact implies
\sigma,c\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. In addition, (1) and (2) are exactly the assumptions (a) and (b) from Condition 2.3 of
[
8
]
in this subcase, so indeed Corollary
3.4
provides a strict generalization of their result. See also Corollary
4.6
where we further extend this result to get an alternate form of the rate function analogous to that of Dawson-Gärtner
[
14
]
.

It is also useful to characterize the way that the limiting equations (
32
),(
35
), and (
37
) act on functions which depend both on time and space. Hence we make the following remark:

Remark 3.6
.

We can alternatively characterize the controlled limiting Equation (
32
) (and analogously the ordinary controlled limiting Equations (
35
) and (
37
)) in terms of how the
Z
acts on
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
, where
U
is an open interval containing
[0,T]
. For Equation (
35
), this characterization is:

(38)

\displaystyle\langle Z_{T},\psi(T,\cdot)\rangle=\int_{0}^{T}\langle Z_{s},\dot{\psi}(s,\cdot)\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{T}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\psi(s,\cdot)\rangle ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}[\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

\displaystyle Z_{0}=0.

This is analogous to the form of the limiting equation seen in
[
25
]
(Remark 2.9) and
[
8
]
(Remark 2.2).

4.
On the form of the rate function

4.1.
Statement and Proof of Equivalent forms of the Rate Function

Here we prove an alternative form of the moderate deviations rate function (
33
), which is analogous to the “negative Sobolev” form of the large deviations rate function for the empirical measure of weakly interacting diffusions found in Theorem 5.1 of the classical work of Dawson-Gärtner
[
14
]
. This is the first time such a form of the rate function has been provided in the moderate deviations setting, both with and without multiscale structure. The result for the specialized case without multiscale structure can be found as Corollary
4.6
below.

A direct connection between the variational form of the large deviations rate function from
[
3
]
and the “negative Sobolev” form of
[
14
]
was recently made for the first time in
[
7
]
Section 5.2. In contrast to the large deviations setting, in the moderate deviations rate function (
34
), we already know the controls
h
are in feedback form, but rather than being feedback controls of the limiting controlled processes
Z
in Equation (
35
), they are feedback controls of the law of large numbers
\mathcal{L}(X)
from Equation (
25
). Moreover, contrast to in the large deviations setting of
[
7
]
, here we handle the dependence of the controls
h
on the parameter
y
do to the multiscale structure and obtaining the “negative Sobolev” form of the rate function uniformly.

In order to state the alternate form of the rate function we first need to recall the following definition:

Definition 4.1
.

(Definition 4.1 in
[
14
]
)
For a compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, we will denote the subspace of
C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
which have compact support contained in
K
by
\mathcal{S}_{K}
.
Let
I
be an interval on the real line. A map
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is called absolutely continuous if for each compact set
K\subset\mathbb{R}
, there exists a neighborhood of
0
in
\mathcal{S}_{K}
and an absolutely continuous function
H_{K}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that

\displaystyle|\langle Z(u),\phi\rangle-\langle Z(v),\phi\rangle|\leq|H_{K}(u)-H_{K}(v)|

for all
u,v\in I
and
\phi\in U_{K}
.

It is also useful to recall the following result:

Lemma 4.2
.

(Lemma 4.2 in
[
14
]
)
Assume the map
Z\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}
is absolutely continuous. Then the real function
\langle Z,\phi\rangle
is absolutely continuous for each
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and the derivative in the distribution sense

\displaystyle\dot{Z}(t)\coloneqq\lim_{h\downarrow 0}h^{-1}[Z(t+h)-Z(t)]

exists for Lebesgue almost-every
t\in I
.

Now we are ready to state the equivalent form of the rate function:

Proposition 4.3
.

Let assumptions
A1)
-
A12)
and
A’13)
hold. Assume also that
\bar{D}(x,\mu)>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, where
\bar{D}
is as in Equation (
24
). Let
r
be as in Equation (
7
). Consider
I^{DG}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
given by:

(39)

\displaystyle I^{DG}(Z)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{4}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{E}[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\phi^{\prime}(X_{t})|^{2}]\neq 0}\frac{\biggl|\langle\dot{Z}_{t}-\bar{L}^{*}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{t})}Z_{t},\phi\rangle\biggr|^{2}}{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\phi^{\prime}(X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]}dt

if
Z(0)=0
,
Z
is absolutely continuous in the sense if Definition
4.1
, and
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
, and
I^{DG}(Z)=+\infty
otherwise. Here
X_{t}
is as in Equation (
25
),
\dot{Z}
is the time derivative of
Z
in the distribution sense from Lemma
4.2
and
\bar{L}^{*}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}_{-w}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-(w+2)}
is the adjoint of
\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{S}_{w+2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{w}
given in Equation (
32
) (using here Lemma
8.6
).

Then
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
from Equation (
3
) satisfies a large deviation principle on the space
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
with speed
a^{-2}(N)
and good rate function
I^{DG}
.

Remark 4.4
.

Note that the assumption that
\bar{D}(x,\mu)>0
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
is not very restrictive. In particular, via the representation for the density of the invariant measure
\pi
given in Equation (
76
), we know it is strictly positive for all
x,\mu
. Then via the representation for
\bar{D}(x,\mu)
given in Equation (
26
), we have that if there is
x,\mu
such that
\bar{D}(x,\mu)=0
, then for that
x,\mu
, we must have

\displaystyle[\tau_{2}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)]^{2}+[\sigma(x,y,\mu)+\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)]^{2}=0

for Lebesgue-almost every
y\in\mathbb{R}
. This will only happen if
\sigma
has a very specific relation to
f,\tau_{1},\tau_{2},b
and hence
\Phi_{y}
.

In order to prove Proposition
4.3
, we first prove the following Lemma, which gives us a form of the rate function analogous to Equation (4.21) in
[
14
]
:

Lemma 4.5
.

Assume the same setup as Proposition
4.3
. For
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
and
Z\in S_{-w}
, define

(40)

\displaystyle F_{Z}(\psi)

\displaystyle=\langle Z_{T},\psi(T,\cdot)\rangle-\int_{0}^{T}\langle Z_{s},\dot{\psi}(s,\cdot)\rangle ds-\int_{0}^{T}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\psi(s,\cdot)\rangle ds

and consider
J\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})\rightarrow[0,+\infty]
given by:

(41)

\displaystyle J(Z)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})}\biggl\{F_{Z}(\psi)-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt\biggr\}

if
Z_{0}=0
and
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
, and
J(Z)=+\infty
otherwise. Here
U
is an open interval containing
[0,T]
. Then
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
satisfies a large deviation principle on the space
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
with speed
a^{-2}(N)
and good rate function
J
.

Proof.

Since by Theorem 1.3.1 in
[
13
]
, the rate function for a sequence of random variables is unique, it suffices to show that
I^{o}=J
, where
I^{o}
is from Corollary
3.3
.
We note that by Remark
3.6
, we can replace
(

P
o

1)
in definition of the multiscale ordinary rate function
I^{o}
by
Z
satisfying Equation (
38
). We will also use the alternative form of
\bar{D}(x,\mu)
provided by Equation (
26
) in Remark
2.5
.

First we show
J\leq I^{o}
. Take
Z
such
I^{o}(Z)<\infty
. Then
P^{o}(Z)
is non-empty, and for any
h\in P^{o}(Z)
and, by Equation (
38
), for any
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle|F_{Z}(\psi)|=\biggl|\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl([\sigma(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))+\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))]h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)

\displaystyle+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)\biggr)\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl([\sigma(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))+\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))]^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+[\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))]^{2}\biggr)\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle\times\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}+|h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds\biggr)^{1/2}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{2}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\biggr)^{1/2}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds\biggr)^{1/2}

so in particular, if
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds=0
, then
F_{Z}(\psi)=0
.
Then, observing that
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
if and only if for any
c\in\mathbb{R}\setminus\{0\}
,
c\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
and that
F_{Z}
is linear, we have:

\displaystyle J(Z)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\neq 0}\biggl\{F_{Z}(\psi)-\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt\biggr\}\vee 0

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\neq 0}\sup_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\biggl\{cF_{Z}(\psi)-c^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt\biggr\}\vee 0

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\neq 0}\frac{|F_{Z}(\psi)|^{2}}{4\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt}.

Returning to the above inequality and squaring both sides, we have

\displaystyle\frac{|F_{Z}(\psi)|^{2}}{2\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt}\leq\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds,

for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
such that
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\neq 0
and all
h\in P^{o}(Z)
. So
J(Z)\leq I^{o}(Z)
.

Now we prove
J\geq I^{o}
.
Assume without loss of generality that
J(Z)\leq C<\infty
. Then, since

\displaystyle J(Z)

\displaystyle=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})}\sup_{c\in\mathbb{R}}\biggl\{cF_{Z}(\psi)-c^{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt\biggr\}=+\infty

if there exists
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})
such that
F_{Z}(\psi)\neq 0
and
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt=0
, we have

\displaystyle J(Z)=\sup_{\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|\psi_{x}(s,X_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\neq 0}\frac{|F_{Z}(\psi)|^{2}}{4\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt}.

This shows that for all
\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R})

(42)

\displaystyle\biggl|F_{Z}(\psi)\biggr|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{C}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\psi_{x}(t,X_{t})|^{2}\biggr]dt\biggr)^{1/2}.

Now we borrow some notation from
[
14
]
(see p. 270-271).
We let for
t\in[0,T]

\nabla_{t},(\cdot,\cdot)_{t},
and
|\cdot|_{t}
be (formally) the Riemannian gradient, inner product, and Riemannian norm in the tangent space of the Riemannian structure on
\mathbb{R}
induced by the diffusion matrix
t\mapsto\bar{D}(\cdot,\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))
, i.e.:

\displaystyle\nabla_{t}f

\displaystyle\coloneqq\bar{D}(\cdot,\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))\frac{df}{dx},\qquad(X,Y)_{t}\coloneqq\bar{D}(\cdot,\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))^{-1}XY,\qquad|X|_{t}\coloneqq(X,X)^{1/2}_{t}.

In particular, note that

\displaystyle|\nabla_{t}f|^{2}_{t}

\displaystyle=\bar{D}(\cdot,\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))|\frac{df}{dx}|^{2}.

Now, as on p.279 in
[
14
]
, we define
L^{2}[0,T]
to be the Hilbert space of measurable maps
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with finite norm

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}\coloneqq\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathcal{L}(X_{t}),|g(t,\cdot)|^{2}_{t}\rangle dt\biggr)^{1/2}=\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))^{-1}|g(t,X_{t})|^{2}]dt\biggr)^{1/2}

and inner product

\displaystyle[g_{1},g_{2}]

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{0}^{T}\langle\mathcal{L}(X_{t}),(g_{1}(t,\cdot),g_{2}(t,\cdot))_{t}\rangle dt=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}[\bar{D}(X_{t},\mathcal{L}(X_{t}))^{-1}g_{1}(t,X_{t})g_{2}(t,X_{t})]dt.

Denote by
L^{2}_{\nabla}[0,T]
