\displaystyle\geq\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})}\biggl\{\inf_{Q\in P^{*}(Z)}\biggl\{\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right)Q(dxdydzds)\biggr\}+F(Z)\biggr\}-\eta

\displaystyle=\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})}\{I(Z)+F(Z)\}-\eta,

where in the second-to-last inequality we used Proposition
8.4
. So Equation (
30
) is established.
∎

10.
Laplace principle Upper Bound and Compactness of Level Sets

We now prove the Laplace principle Upper Bound and, under the additional assumption of
A’13)
, compactness of level sets.

Proposition 10.1
.

Under assumptions
A1)
-
A13)
, the Laplace principle Upper Bound (
31
) holds.

Proof.

We use the ordinary formulation
I^{o}
from Equation (
34
). We take
\eta>0
,
w
as in Equation (
6
),
\tau\geq w
,
F\in C_{b}(C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\tau}))
, and
Z^{*}
such that

\displaystyle I(Z^{*})+F(Z^{*})\leq\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})}\mathbb{E}\biggl[I(Z)+F(Z)\biggr]+\frac{\eta}{2}.

Then we can find
h\in P^{o}(Z^{*})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds\leq I(Z^{*})+\frac{\eta}{2}.

Then since
\nu(\Gamma\times A\times B)\coloneqq\int_{\Gamma}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{A}(x)\int_{\mathbb{R}}\mathbbm{1}_{B}(y)\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\mathcal{L}(X_{s})(dx)ds
,
\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}(U),A,B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})
is a finite Borel measure on
U\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
, by Corollary 22.38 (1) in
[
16
]
, we can take
\{\psi^{k}_{j}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\subset C^{\infty}_{c}(U\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
such that
\psi^{k}_{j}\rightarrow h_{j}
in
L^{2}(U\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},\nu)
for
j\in\{1,2\}
. Here we let
U
be any open interval containing
[0,T]
and assume
\nu(\Gamma\times A\times B)
is
0
when
\Gamma\cap[0,T]=\emptyset
.

Then letting
\tilde{u}^{N}_{i,k}(s,\omega)=\psi^{k}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s}(\omega),\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s}(\omega))
, where
(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})
are as in Equation (
55
) but controlled by
\frac{\tilde{u}^{N}_{i,k}(s)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N}_{i,k}(s)|^{2}\biggr]ds

\displaystyle=\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\psi_{k}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s})|^{2}\biggr]ds\leq T\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\psi_{k}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}

for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and in fact

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N}_{i,k}(s)|^{2}ds\leq T\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\psi_{k}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}

for each
k\in\mathbb{N}
(so the supposition (
53
) holds with this choice of controls).

Letting
(\tilde{Z}^{N,k},Q^{N,k})
be as in Equations (
54
) and (
56
) with this choice of controls, we want to establish that
(\tilde{Z}^{N,k},Q^{N,k})
converges in distribution as a sequence of
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-m})\times M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
-valued random variables to
(\tilde{Z}^{k},Q^{k})
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, where
Q^{k}\in P^{*}(\tilde{Z}^{k})
(this is immediate since we prove this for all
L^{2}
controls in Proposition
8.4
) and such that

(70)

\displaystyle Q^{k}(A\times B\times C\times\Gamma)=\int_{\Gamma}\int_{A}\int_{C}\delta_{\psi^{k}(s,x,y)}(C)\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\mathcal{L}(X_{s})(dx)ds,\forall A,B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}),C\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}([0,T]).

By the weak-sense uniqueness established in Proposition
8.8
, this determines each
\tilde{Z}^{k}
almost surely to be the unique element of
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-m})
satisfying Equation (
32
) with
Q^{k}
in the place of
Q
.

Then we will send
k\rightarrow\infty
and show
(\tilde{Z}^{k},Q^{k})
converges to
(\tilde{Z},Q)
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})\times M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
, where
Q\in P^{*}(\tilde{Z})
and

(71)

\displaystyle Q(A\times B\times C\times\Gamma)=\int_{\Gamma}\int_{A}\int_{C}\delta_{h(s,x,y)}(C)\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\mathcal{L}(X_{s})(dx)ds,\forall A,B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}),C\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}^{2}),\Gamma\in\mathcal{B}([0,T]).

Then by the weak-sense uniqueness established in Proposition
8.8
, we have
\tilde{Z}\overset{d}{=}Z^{*}
. By reverse Fatou’s lemma:

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}-a^{2}(N)\log\mathbb{E}\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)}F(Z^{N})\biggr)

\displaystyle=\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\inf_{\tilde{u}^{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\left(|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,2}_{i}(s)|^{2}\right)ds+F(\tilde{Z}^{N})\biggr]\text{ by Equation \eqref{eq:varrepfunctionalsBM}}

\displaystyle\leq\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\left(|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i,k}(s)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,2}_{i,k}(s)|^{2}\right)ds+F(\tilde{Z}^{N,k})\biggr],\forall k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q^{N,k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)+F(\tilde{Z}^{N,k})\biggr],\forall k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q^{k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)+F(\tilde{Z}^{k})\biggr],\forall k\in\mathbb{N}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|\psi^{k}(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds+\mathbb{E}\biggl[F(\tilde{Z}^{k})\biggr],\forall k\in\mathbb{N}

Then sending
k\rightarrow\infty
and using the
L^{2}
convergence of
\psi^{k}
to
h
and the boundedness
F
and convergence of
\tilde{Z}^{k}
to
Z^{*}
, we get

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\rightarrow\infty}-a^{2}(N)\log\mathbb{E}\exp\biggl(-\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)}F(Z^{N})\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}|h(s,X_{s},y)|^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds+\mathbb{E}\biggl[F(Z^{*})\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq I(Z^{*})+F(Z^{*})+\frac{\eta}{2}

\displaystyle\leq\inf_{Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})}\mathbb{E}\biggl[I(Z)+F(Z)\biggr]+\eta

so Equation (
31
) will be established.

Looking at the proof of Proposition
8.3
, to see
(\tilde{Z}^{N,k},Q^{N,k})
converges to
(\tilde{Z}^{k},Q^{k})
where
Q^{k}\in P^{*}(\tilde{Z}^{k})
satisfies Equation (
70
). we just need to establish that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}\sigma(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)Q^{N,k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}[\tau_{1}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(x)Q^{N,k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)

converges in distribution to

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left([\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\right)\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

for all
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
. Fix
k\in\mathbb{N}
and
\phi
and
t
. We have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}\sigma(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)Q^{N,k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)

\displaystyle+\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,t]}[\tau_{1}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(x)Q^{N,k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\tau_{1}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})+\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\tau_{2}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\right]\Phi_{y}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s}\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})ds

Then using Proposition
6.4
with

\displaystyle F(s,x,y,\mu)=\sigma(x,y,\mu)\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mu)\psi^{k}_{2}(s,x,y)]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)

using that
s
only appears as a parameter, in the same way as
x
, so that the same proof holds (using also the assumed bound on the time derivative of
\Xi
in
A8)
), we get that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sigma(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})ds

\displaystyle+\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\left[\tau_{1}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})+\right.

\displaystyle\qquad\left.+\tau_{2}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\right]\Phi_{y}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})ds

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})

\displaystyle\quad+[\tau_{1}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)]\times

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Phi_{y}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\pi(dy;\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s})ds\biggr|\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\epsilon

Then noting that

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\sigma(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})

\displaystyle+[\tau_{1}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y)]\times

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Phi_{y}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s},y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\pi(dy;\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s})ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)\phi^{\prime}(x)+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)+\tau_{2}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,x,y)]\times

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Phi_{y}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)\pi(dy;x,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s})\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s}(dx)ds

and using that the integrand of the first two integrals above is bounded by Assumptions
A1)
,
A5)
,and
A6)
and continuous in
\mathbb{W}_{2}
by Assumption
A12)
, along with the convergence of
\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s}
to
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
from Lemma
8.2
, we have by dominated convergence theorem (invoking here Skorokhod’s representation theorem to assume
\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s}
to
\mathcal{L}(X_{s})
almost surely as in Proposition
8.3
) and Theorem A.3.18 in
[
13
]
:

\displaystyle\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)\phi^{\prime}(x)+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)+\tau_{2}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\psi^{k}_{2}(s,x,y)]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Phi_{y}(x,y,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s})\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)\pi(dy;x,\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{s})\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,k}_{s}(dx)ds

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)\phi^{\prime}(x)+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{2}(s,x,y)]

\displaystyle\hskip 142.26378pt\times\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\mathcal{L}(X_{s})(dx)ds\biggr|\biggr]=0

so by triangle inequality, the desired convergence is shown.

Now we seek to establish that
(\tilde{Z}^{k},Q^{k})
converges to
(\tilde{Z},Q)
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})\times M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
where
Q\in P^{*}(\tilde{Z})
and
Q
satisfies (
71
).

We first prove precompactness. We have since
\psi^{k}\rightarrow h
in
L^{2}(U\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R},\nu)
,

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q^{k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)=

\displaystyle\quad=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(|\psi^{k}_{1}(s,x,y)|^{2}+|\psi^{k}_{2}(s,x,y)|^{2}\right)\pi(dy;x,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\mathcal{L}(X_{s})(dx)ds<\infty.

Moreover, by
(

P
enumi

3)
and
(

P
enumi

4)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(y^{2}+|x|^{2}\right)Q^{k}(dx,dy,dz,ds)=\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}y^{2}\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))+|X_{s}|^{2}\biggr]ds<\infty,\forall k\in\mathbb{N},

where here we have used that
\pi(\cdot;x,\mu)
from Equation (
20
) has bounded moments of all orders uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and that for
X_{s}
from Equation (
25
),
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[|X_{s}|^{2}]<\infty,
which follows easily from the fact that
\bar{\gamma}
and
\bar{D}
are bounded as per Assumption
A13)
. Thus, via the same tightness function used for Proposition
7.10
,
\{Q^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.

To see that
\{\tilde{Z}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact, we use that for each
k

(\tilde{Z}^{k},Q^{k})
must satisfy Equation (
32
). That is, for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and
t\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{k}_{s},\phi\rangle ds

\displaystyle\langle B^{k}_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)Q_{t}^{k}(dx,dy,dz).

Here
Q^{k}_{t}\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
is such that
Q^{k}(dx,dy,dz,dt)=Q^{k}_{t}(dx,dy,dz)dt
. We can see that
B^{k}_{t}\in\mathcal{S}_{-(m+2)}
for almost every
t\in[0,T]
, and in fact

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert B^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}ds

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{m+2}=1}|\langle B^{k}_{s},\phi\rangle|^{2}ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{m+2}=1}\biggl\{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right)Q^{k}_{s}(dx,dy,dz)|\phi|^{2}_{1}\biggr\}ds

\displaystyle\leq\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\sup_{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{m+2}=1}\biggl\{\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right)Q^{k}_{s}(dx,dy,dz)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}^{2}_{m+2}\biggr\}ds

\displaystyle=\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\left(|z_{1}|^{2}+|z_{2}|^{2}\right)Q^{k}_{s}(dx,dy,dz)ds<\infty

Thus, by the proof of Theorem 2.5.2 in
[
37
]
, it suffices to show that for fixed
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
,
\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t},\phi\rangle
is relatively compact in
C([0,T];\mathbb{R})
, and
\tilde{Z}^{k}
is uniformly
(m+2)
-continuous to get precompactness of
\tilde{Z}^{k}
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
for
w>m+2
sufficiently large that the canonical embedding
\mathcal{S}_{-m-2}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-w}
is Hilbert-Schmidt (see Equation (
6
)).

We have that, in the same way as the proof of Proposition
8.8
(using here that
\tilde{Z}^{k}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-m})
),

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t}\right\rVert}_{-(m+2)}^{2}

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},L^{*}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s}\rangle_{-(m+2)}ds+2\int_{0}^{t}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},B^{k}_{s}\rangle_{-(m+2)}ds

\displaystyle\leq C\int_{0}^{t}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}ds+2\int_{0}^{t}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}_{-(m+2)}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert B^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}_{-(m+2)}ds\text{ by Cauchy Schwarz and Lemma \ref{lemma:4.32BW}}

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl\{\int_{0}^{t}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert B^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}ds\biggr\}

so by Gronwall’s inequality,

\displaystyle\sup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}\leq C(T).

This gives then that for
t_{1},t_{2}\in[0,T]
and
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
:

\displaystyle|\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t_{2}},\phi\rangle-\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t_{1}},\phi\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq 2|t_{2}-t_{1}|\biggl\{\int_{0}^{T}|\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle|^{2}ds+\int_{0}^{T}|\langle B^{k}_{s},\phi\rangle|^{2}ds\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq 2|t_{2}-t_{1}|\biggl\{\int_{0}^{T}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s}\right\rVert}_{-(m+2)}^{2}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\right\rVert}^{2}_{m+2}ds+\int_{0}^{T}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert B^{N}_{s}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(m+2)}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}^{2}_{m+2}ds\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq 2|t_{2}-t_{1}|C(T)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}^{2}_{m+4}\text{ by Lemma \ref{lem:barLbounded},}

and precompactness of
\{\tilde{Z}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
is established.

Taking a convergent subsequence, which we do not relabel in the notation, we call its limit
(Z,Q)
. The fact that
(

P
enumi

2)
-
(

P
enumi

4)
in the definition of
P^{*}(Z)
are satisfied follows in the exact same way as Proposition
10.2
. It thus remains to show that
(Z,Q)
satisfies Equation (
32
) with
Q
given in Equation (
71
). At this point, by Proposition
8.8
, we will have the limit is uniquely identified for every subsequence, and hence the lemma is proved. By a density argument, it suffices to show that for each
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t},\phi\rangle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})

\displaystyle+[\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\biggr)\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds.

We have by dominated convergence theorem,
L^{2}
convergence of
\psi^{k}
to

, and that under Assumption

\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{w},\forall s\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{t},\phi\rangle=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\biggl\{\int_{0}^{t}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{k}_{s},\phi\rangle ds\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\langle\tilde{Z}^{k}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})

\displaystyle+[\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\psi^{k}_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\biggr)\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})

\displaystyle+[\tau_{1}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{1}(s,X_{s},y)+\tau_{2}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))h_{2}(s,X_{s},y)]\Phi_{y}(X_{s},y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(X_{s})\biggr)\pi(dy;X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\biggr]ds

as desired.
∎

Proposition 10.2
.

Under assumptions
A1)
-
A12)
and
A’13)
,
I
given in Theorems
3.1
/
3.2
is a good rate function on
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
for
r>w+2
as in Equation (
7
).

Proof.

We need to show that for any
L>0
,

\displaystyle\Theta_{L}\coloneqq\{Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}I(Z)\leq L\}

is compact in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
.

Let
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\subset\Theta_{L}
. Then by the form of
I
, for each
N\in\mathbb{N}
, there exists
Q^{N}\in P^{*}(Z^{N})
such that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q^{N}(dx,dy,dz,ds)\leq L+\frac{1}{N}

and by
(

P
enumi

3)
and
(

P
enumi

4)
, we have as with the
Q^{k}
’s in the proof of Proposition
9.1

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(y^{2}+|x|^{2}\right)Q^{N}(dx,dy,dz,ds)<\infty.

Thus by the same tightness function used for Proposition
7.10
,
\{Q^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is tight in
M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
.

Taking a subsequence of
\{Q^{N}\}
which converges to some
Q\in M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
(which we do not relabel in the notation), define
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
to be the unique solution to Equation (
32
) with this choice of
Q
. Here we are using that by the proof of Proposition
10.1
such a solution exists and that by Proposition
8.8
it is unique - see the discussion before Lemma 4.10 in
[
8
]
. We claim that
(Z^{N},Q^{N})
converges to
(Z,Q)
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})\times M_{T}(\mathbb{R}^{4})
and
Q\in P^{*}(Z)
. At this point we will have that since
Z^{N}
has a limit,
\Theta_{L}
is precompact, and by the version of Fatou’s lemma from Theorem A.3.12 in
[
13
]
:

\displaystyle I(Z)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q(dx,dy,dz,ds)\leq\liminf_{N\rightarrow\infty}\frac{1}{2}\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times[0,T]}\left(z_{1}^{2}+z_{2}^{2}\right)Q^{N}(dx,dy,dz,ds)\leq L

so
\Theta_{L}
is closed, and hence compact. Note that we have
I(Z^{N})<\infty
implies
Z^{N}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w}),\forall N\in\mathbb{N}
and by definition
Z\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
. Thus if we could show convergence of
Z^{N}\rightarrow Z
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
, we would have compactness of level sets of
I
as a rate function on
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
. However, such convergence is not immediately obvious, hence the need for the additional assumption
A’13)
.

To see that
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
is precompact, we have that since
Q^{N}\in P^{*}(Z^{N})
, for each
N

(Z^{N},Q^{N})
must satisfy Equation (
32
). That is, for
\phi\in\mathcal{S}
and
t\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\langle Z^{N}_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z^{N}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{N}_{s},\phi\rangle ds

\displaystyle\langle B^{N}_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)Q_{t}^{N}(dx,dy,dz).

Thus precompactness of
\{Z^{N}\}_{N\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-r})
follows in the exact same way as precompactness of
\{\tilde{Z}^{k}\}_{k\in\mathbb{N}}
in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
in the proof of Proposition
10.1
, but replacing
m
by
w
. Note that there we knew that
\tilde{Z}^{k}
was in
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-m})
for each
k
, where here we only know
Z^{N}\in C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-w})
for each
N
.
Along the way, we get:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert Z^{N}_{t}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(w+2)}\leq C(T).

To see that
Q\in P^{*}(Z)
, we identify the point-wise limit of
\langle Z^{N},\phi\rangle
to satisfy the desired equation, i.e. (
32
) with our specific choice of
Q
. This uniquely characterizes the limit along the whole sequence by Lemma
8.8
. This gives
(

P
enumi

1)
.
(

P
enumi

2)
follows immediately from Fatou’s lemma.
(

P
enumi

3)
and
(

P
enumi

4)
follow from convergence of the measure implying convergence of the marginals and uniqueness of the decomposition into stochastic kernels (see
[
13
]
Theorems A.4.2 and A.5.4).

To see (
32
) with our specific choice of
Q
holds, we may by a density argument consider fixed
\phi\in C^{\infty}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
. Then:

\displaystyle\langle Z_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle Z^{N}_{t},\phi\rangle=\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\biggl\{\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z^{N}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{N}_{s},\phi\rangle ds\biggr\}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\langle Z^{N}_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{N}_{s},\phi\rangle ds

\displaystyle\text{ (by boundedness of }\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert Z^{N}_{t}\right\rVert}^{2}_{-(w+2)}\text{ and Dominated Convergence Theorem)}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{t}\langle Z_{s},\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\rangle ds+\int_{0}^{t}\langle B_{s},\phi\rangle ds,\text{ since under assumption \ref{assumption:limitingcoefficientsregularityratefunction} $\bar{L}_{\mathcal{L}(X_{s})}\phi\in\mathcal{S}_{r},\forall s\in[0,T]$.}

Here

\displaystyle\langle B_{t},\phi\rangle

\displaystyle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}}\biggl(\sigma(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}\phi^{\prime}(x)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt+[\tau_{1}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{1}+\tau_{2}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))z_{2}]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))\phi^{\prime}(x)\biggr)Q_{t}(dx,dy,dz),

and to pass to the second limit, we use that the integrand appearing in
\int_{0}^{t}\langle B^{N}_{s},\phi\rangle ds
is bounded by
C[|z_{1}|+|z_{2}|]
, and hence is uniformly integrable with respect to
Q^{N}
.
∎

11.
Conclusions and Future Work

In this paper we have derived a moderate deviations principle for the empirical measure of a fully coupled multiscale system of weakly interacting particles in the joint limit as number of particles increases and averaging due to the multiscale structure takes over. Using weak convergence methods we have derived a variational form of the rate function and have rigorously shown that the rate function can take equivalent forms analogous to the one derived in the seminal paper
[
14
]
.

In this paper we have assumed that the particles are in dimension one. It is of great interest to extend this work in the multidimensional case. One source of difficulty here is that in higher dimensions we would probably have to consider a different space for the fluctuation process to live in (see, e.g.
[
49
]
and
[
64
]
). This is because in higher dimensions the result that for each
v
, there is
w\geq v
such that
\mathcal{S}_{-v}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}_{-w}
is Hilbert-Schmidt breaks down, and the bound (
9
) no longer holds true. See
[
15
]
Section 5.1 for a further discussion of this. The trade-off with using these alternative spaces is that they often require higher moments of the particles and limiting McKean-Vlasov Equation in order to establish tightness - see, e.g. Section 4.7 in
[
49
]
, where the proofs depend crucially on Lemma 3.1 (even in one dimension this would require having bounded
8
’th moments of the controlled particles
\tilde{X}^{i,N,\epsilon}
, with the required number of moments increasing with the dimension). This would seem to require strong assumptions on the coefficients in Equation (
1
) even in the absence of multiscale structure, since the controls are a priori only bounded in
L^{2}
.

Another potentially interesting direction is to derive the moderate deviations principle for the stochastic current. See
[
25
]
for some related results in the direction of large deviations for an interacting particle system in the joint mean field and small-noise limit. Also, we are hopeful that the results of this paper can also be used for the construction of provably-efficient importance sampling schemes for the computation of rare events for statistics of weakly interacting diffusions that are relevant to the moderate-deviations scaling. Lastly, as we also mentioned in the introduction, we believe that the results of this paper can be used to study dynamical questions related to phase transitions in the spirit of
[
11
]
.

Appendix B
A priori Bounds on Moments of the Controlled Process (
55
)

In this Appendix, we fix any controls satisfying the bound (
53
) and provide moment bounds on the fast component of the controlled particles (
55
). These are needed, among other places, to handle possible growth lack of boundedness in
y
of functions appearing in the remainders in the ergodic-type theorems of Section
6
.

Lemma B.1
.

Under assumptions
A1)
-
A2)
,
A4)
, and
A5)
, we have there is
C\geq 0
such that:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\leq C+|\eta^{y}|^{2}.

Proof.

By Itô’s formula, we have, letting
C\geq 0
be any constant independent of
N
which may change from line to line and
(i)
denote the argument
(\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s},\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s},\tilde{\mu}^{\epsilon,N})
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]=|\eta^{y}|^{2}+\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(2f(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}+\tau_{1}^{2}(i)+\tau_{2}^{2}(i)\biggr)\biggr]ds+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[g(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\biggl(\tau_{1}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}+\tau_{2}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}\biggr)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}ds\biggr]+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{1}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{2}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dB^{i}_{s}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]ds+\frac{Ct}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[g(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\biggl(\tau_{1}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}+\tau_{2}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}\biggr)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}ds\biggr]+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{1}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{2}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dB^{i}_{s}\biggr]\text{ by }\eqref{eq:fdecayimplication}

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{2}-\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]ds+\frac{Ct}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]ds+\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}\frac{t}{\epsilon}

\displaystyle+\frac{|\tau_{1}|^{2}_{\infty}\vee|\tau_{2}|^{2}_{\infty}}{\epsilon a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}\biggr]+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{1}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\tau_{2}(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dB^{i}_{s}\biggr]

at which point it becomes clear that applying Burkholder Davis Gundy inequality, taking
\epsilon
small enough that the
\frac{-\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}
term dominates, and using the bound (
53
), that
\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]<\infty
for each
N
, so by boundedness of
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
the stochastic integrals are true martingales, and hence vanish in expectation. Note that in the above we are using the boundedness of
\tau_{1},\tau_{2}
from Assumption
A1)
and of
g
from Assumption
A5)
.

So, returning to the initial equality:

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(2f(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}+\tau_{1}^{2}(i)+\tau_{2}^{2}(i)\biggr)\biggr]+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[g(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{2}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\tau_{1}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}+\tau_{2}(i)\frac{\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)}{a(N)\sqrt{N}}\biggr)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{4\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{\infty}^{2}}{\beta}+\frac{\beta}{4\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}]+\frac{4}{\beta a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\tau_{1}(i)\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)+\tau_{2}(i)\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle+\frac{\beta}{4\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{4\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{\infty}^{2}}{\beta}+\frac{8\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau_{1}\right\rVert}_{\infty}^{2}\vee\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau_{2}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}}{\beta a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)|^{2}\biggr]

Where in the first inequality we used the consequence (
19
) of Assumption
A2)
. Now, recalling that if
g^{\prime}(s)\leq-\gamma g(s)+f(s),\forall s\in[0,t],
then
g(t)\leq\int_{0}^{t}f(s)e^{-\gamma(t-s)}ds+e^{-\gamma t}g(0)
, we set
g(t)=\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr],\gamma=\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}},f(t)=\frac{CN}{\epsilon^{2}}+\frac{4\mathinner{\!\left\lVert g\right\rVert}_{\infty}^{2}N}{\beta}+\frac{8\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau_{1}\right\rVert}_{\infty}^{2}\vee\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau_{2}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}}{\beta a^{2}(N)}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(t)|^{2}\biggr]

and get

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\leq e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}CN(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds+N|\eta^{y}|^{2}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}CN(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}+|\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)|^{2}\biggr]ds+N|\eta^{y}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}CN(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)}+N|\eta^{y}|^{2}\text{ by the bound }\eqref{eq:controlassumptions0}

\displaystyle=e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}CN(1+\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}})\frac{2\epsilon^{2}}{\beta}[e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}-1]+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)}+N|\eta^{y}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq CN(1+\epsilon^{2})+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)}+N|\eta^{y}|^{2}

where
C
is a constant changing from line to line which is independent of
t
and
N
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq C(1+\epsilon^{2}+\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)N})+|\eta^{y}|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C+|\eta^{y}|^{2}

since
\epsilon\downarrow 0
and
a(N)\sqrt{N}\rightarrow\infty
.
∎

Lemma B.2
.

Under assumptions
A1)
-
A2)
,
A4)
,and
A5)
, we have:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\leq|\eta^{y}|^{2}+C(\rho)\biggl[1+\epsilon^{-\rho}+\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)N}\biggr]

for all
\rho\in(0,2)
.

Proof.

The proof follows along the lines of that of Lemma B.4 in
[
45
]
and thus it is omitted here. Note that this is where the near-Ornstein–Uhlenbeck structure assumed in (
16
) plays an important role.
∎

Lemma B.3
.

Under assumptions
A1)
-
A2)
,
A4)
, and
A5)
, we have:

\displaystyle\sup_{N\in\mathbb{N}}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]<\infty.

Proof.

The proof is very similar to Lemma
B.1
, but we need in addition to use the result of Lemma
B.2
. Because of the similarities, we assume wlog that
\tau_{2}=g=0
and label
\tau_{1}
as
\tau
and
\tilde{u}^{N,1}_{i}(s)
and
\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)
. Then for every
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]=

\displaystyle=|\eta^{y}|^{4}+\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl\{4\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(f(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}+\tau^{2}(i)\biggr)\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+4\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\frac{1}{\epsilon a(N)\sqrt{N}}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr]ds

\displaystyle+8\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N}\tau^{2}(i)|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]ds+\frac{4}{\epsilon}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{t}\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\tau(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}dW^{i}_{s}\biggr]\biggr\},

where we used the initial conditions are IID. By the same method as in Lemma
B.1
, we can see that the martingale term vanishes for each
N
and
t
. Then we get

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl\{4\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}}\biggl(f(i)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}+\tau^{2}(i)\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)\frac{1}{\epsilon a(N)\sqrt{N}}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(t)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr]+8\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N}\tau^{2}(i)|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)\biggl(-\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}+\frac{2}{\epsilon^{2}}\tau^{2}(i)\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+4\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)\frac{1}{\epsilon a(N)\sqrt{N}}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(t)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr]+8\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{\epsilon^{2}N}\tau^{2}(i)|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\biggr\}

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\beta}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]+\frac{4}{\epsilon a(N)\sqrt{N}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(t)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(t)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr]\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr].

Here we again used the implication (
19
) of Assumption
A2)
and the boundedness of
\tau
from
A1)
. Then by the comparison theorem from the proof of Lemma
B.1
, we have for all
t\in[0,T],N\in\mathbb{N}
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{t}|^{2}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]\leq

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}+\frac{C}{\epsilon^{2}}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\int_{0}^{t}e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}+C\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\tau(i)\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}\biggr)^{2}\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}+C\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}e^{-\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}t}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)|^{2}\biggr)\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\biggr]e^{\frac{\beta}{2\epsilon^{2}}s}ds

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}+C\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{u}^{N}_{i}(s)|^{2}\biggr)ds\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq|\eta^{y}|^{4}+C\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N}\biggr]\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+\frac{C}{a^{2}(N)N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\biggl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|\tilde{Y}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}|^{2}\biggr)\biggr]\text{ by the bound \eqref{eq:controlassumptions}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\eta^{y},\rho)[1+\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)N}+\frac{1}{a^{4}(N)N^{2}}+\frac{1}{a^{2}(N)N\epsilon^{\rho}}]

for any
\rho\in(0,2)
by Lemmas
B.1
and
B.2
, where the constant
C
is independent of
N
. Then taking
\rho\in(0,2)
such that such that
\epsilon^{\rho/2}a(N)\sqrt{N}\rightarrow\lambda\in(0,\infty]
and using
a(N)\sqrt{N}\rightarrow\infty
, all the terms in the bound which depend on
N
are bounded as
N\rightarrow\infty
, so we get a bound independent of
N
and
t
, and the result is proved.
∎

Appendix C
Regularity of the Poisson Equations

As discussed in Remark
2.6
, there is a current gap in the literature regarding rates of polynomial growth of derivatives of the Poisson equations used in Section
6
.
Nevertheless, it is important to verify that the assumptions imposed on these solutions in Section
2
are non-empty.
For the reasons outlined in Remark
2.6
, we handle the case of the 1D Poisson equations from Equations (
22
),(
64
) and the Multi-Dimensional Poisson Equations (
28
) and (
63
),
separately in Subsections
C.1
and
C.2
below. In Subsection
C.3
we provide specific examples where the Assumptions in Section
2
hold.

C.1.
Results for the 1-Dimensional Poisson Equation

Throughout this subsection we assume
A1)
and
A2)
. Recall the frozen generator
L_{x,\mu}
from Equation (
18
), the invariant measure
\pi
from Equation (
20
), the multi-index derivative notation and associated spaces of functions from Definitions
2.1
and
2.4
, and the definition of
a
from Equation (
18
).

Lemma C.1
.

Consider
B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
continuous such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

and
|B(x,y,\mu)|=O(|y|^{q_{B}})
for
q_{B}\in\mathbb{R}
uniformly in
x,\mu
as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
.
Then there exists a unique classical solution
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}u(x,y,\mu)=B(x,y,\mu)

such that
u
is continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0
, and
u
has at most polynomial growth as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
.

In addition,

\displaystyle|u(x,y,\mu)|

\displaystyle=O(|y|^{q_{B}})\text{ for }q_{B}\neq 0;\text{ if }q_{B}=0,\text{ then }|u(x,y,\mu)|=O(\ln(|y|)

\displaystyle|u_{y}(x,y,\mu)|

\displaystyle=O(|y|^{q_{B}-1}),\quad|u_{yy}(x,y,\mu)|=O(|y|^{q_{B}})

as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
uniformly in
x,y,\mu
.

Furthermore if
B(x,y,\mu)
is Lipschitz continuous in
y
uniformly in
x,\mu
(so that necessarily
q_{B}\leq 1
), then so are
u,u_{y},u_{yy}
.

Proof.

This follows by Proposition A.4 in
[
68
]
, since in our setting Condition 2.1 (i) holds with
\alpha=1
and assumption A.3 holds with
\theta=1
. The statement about Lipschitz continuity of
u
and its derivatives follows from the Lipschitz continuity of
a,f
under assumptions
A1)
and
A2)
and Theorem 9.19 in
[
71
]
.
∎

Lemma C.2
.

Consider
h\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. Suppose
h
in jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
and grows at most polynomially in
y
uniformly in
x,\mu
. Then

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu_{1})\pi(dy;x,\mu_{1})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu_{2})\pi(dy;x,\mu_{2})=

(72)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu_{1})-h(x,y,\mu_{2})-[\mathcal{L}_{x,\mu_{1}}-\mathcal{L}_{x,\mu_{2}}]v(x,y,\mu_{2})\pi(dy;x,\mu_{1})

for all
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,
and

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x_{1},y,\mu)\pi(dy;x_{1},\mu)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x_{2},y,\mu)\pi(dy;x_{2},\mu)=

(73)

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x_{1},y,\mu)-h(x_{2},y,\mu)-[\mathcal{L}_{x_{1},\mu}-\mathcal{L}_{x_{2},\mu}]v(x_{2},y,\mu)\pi(dy;x_{1},\mu)

for all
x_{1},x_{2}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Here
v
solves

(74)

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{x,\mu}v(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=h(x,y,\mu)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,\bar{y},\mu)\pi(d\bar{y};x,\mu).

Consider also
\mathcal{L}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}
the differential operator acting on
\phi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi(y)=D^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}f(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime}(y)+D^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}a(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime\prime}(y).

Assume that for some complete collection of multi-indices
\bm{\zeta}
, that
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, and that
v_{y},v_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
, where
\bm{\zeta}^{\prime}
is obtained from removing any multi-indices which contain the maximal first and second values from
\bm{\zeta}
. Then for any multi-index
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
:

(75)

\displaystyle D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}h(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]-\right.

\displaystyle\quad\left.-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{l}\sum_{\bm{p}_{k}}C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}\mathcal{L}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]D^{(n-k,l-j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k}))}v(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k}}]\right)\pi(dy;x,\mu)

where here
\bm{p}_{k}\in\binom{\{1,...,n\}}{k}
with
\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k}=\{1,...,n\}\setminus\bm{p}_{k}
, for
\bm{p}_{k}=\{p_{1},...,p_{k}\}
, the argument
[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]
denotes
[z_{p_{1}},...,z_{p_{k}}]
, and
\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k})\in\mathbb{N}^{k}
is determined by
\bm{\beta}=(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{n})
by
\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k})=(\alpha_{1},...,\alpha_{k})
,
\alpha_{j}=\beta_{p_{j}},j\in\{1,...,k\}
, and similarly for
\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k})
. Also here
C_{(\bm{p}_{0},0,n,l)}=0
, and
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}>0,C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}\in\mathbb{N}
for
(k,j)\in\mathbb{N}^{2},(k,j)\neq(0,0)
, (see Remark
C.3
for the exact definition of these constants).

The same result holds replacing
D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
with
\bm{\delta}^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
if in addition we assume
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
v_{y},v_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
. In this setting we will denote by
\mathcal{L}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k})),\bm{\delta}}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]
is the differential operator acting on
\phi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k})),\bm{\delta}}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi(y)=\bm{\delta}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}f(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime}(y)+\bm{\delta}^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}a(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime\prime}(y).

Proof.

The proofs of (
C.2
), (
C.2
) and of (
75
) for the Lions derivatives is the content of Lemma A.4 in
[
5
]
.

For the linear functional derivatives, we can use the exact same proof as in Lemma A.4 of
[
5
]
. Though it is not immediately obvious from the definition of the linear functional derivative that standard properties of derivatives such as chain and product rule apply, we can use Proposition 5.44/Remark 5.47 along with the representation (5.50) from Proposition 5.51 in
[
9
]
to see that these properties are inherited from the Lions derivative (which is defined via lifting and using a Fréchet derivative). Note that the needed uniform in
\mu
Lipschitz continuity assumption for the Lions derivatives of
a,b,h
needed for Proposition 5.51 is already implied by the definition of
\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Then we get

\displaystyle\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu)\pi(y;x,\mu)dy[z]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}h(x,y,\mu)[z]-\mathcal{L}^{(1,0,0),\bm{\delta}}_{x,\mu}[z]v(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu),

for all
x,z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
, and can induct on
l,n
in the same way as is done in Lemma A.4 of
[
5
]
. The details are omitted for brevity.

∎

Remark C.3
.

The non-negative integers
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}
in the statement of Lemma
C.2
can be iteratively computed according to the following rules:

\displaystyle C_{(\bm{p}_{0},0,0,0)}

\displaystyle=0

To go up in
l
(taking an
x
derivative), we have for any
l,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{N}\ni j\leq l+1
,
\mathbb{N}\ni k\leq n
, and
\bm{p}_{k}\in\binom{\{1,...,n\}}{k}
:

\displaystyle C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l+1)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}C_{(\bm{p}_{k},l,n,l)},&\text{ if }j=l+1\\
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},0,n,l)},&\text{ if }j=0\\
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},1,n,l)}+1,&\text{ if }j=1\\
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j-1,n,l)}+C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)},&\text{ otherwise}\\
\end{cases}

To go up in
n
(taking a measure derivative), we have for any
l,n\in\mathbb{N}
,
\mathbb{N}\ni j\leq l
,
\mathbb{N}\ni k\leq n+1
, and
\bm{p}_{k}\in\binom{\{1,...,n+1\}}{k}

\displaystyle C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n+1,l)}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}C_{(\{1,...,n\},j,n,l)}&\text{ if }k=n+1\\
C_{(\bm{p}_{0},j,n,l)}&\text{ if }k=0\\
\mathbbm{1}_{\bm{p}_{1}=\{n+1\}}+C_{(\bm{p}_{1},j,n,l)}\mathbbm{1}_{\bm{p}_{1}\neq\{n+1\}}&\text{ if }k=1\\
C_{(\bm{p}_{k}\setminus\{n+1\},j,n,l)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{n+1\}\in\bm{p}_{k}}+C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}\mathbbm{1}_{\{n+1\}\not\in\bm{p}_{k}},&\text{ otherwise}.\\
\end{cases}

Lemma C.4
.

Consider
B\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
continuous such that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Suppose that for some complete collection of multi-indices
\bm{\zeta}
that
B,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.
Then for the unique classical solution
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
to

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}u(x,y,\mu)=B(x,y,\mu)

such that
u
is continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
,
\int_{\mathbb{R}}u(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0
, and
u
has at most polynomial growth as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
(which exists by Lemma
C.1
),

(1)

u,u_{y},u_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

(2)

If
B,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, then
u,u_{y},u_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

(3)

If
B,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, then
u,u_{y},u_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Moreover, if we suppose that for all multi-indices
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
,
q_{f}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1
and
q_{a}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
(using here the notation of (
13
)), we have control on the growth rate of the derivatives of
u
in terms of those of
B
. In particular, for any
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
:

\displaystyle q_{u}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq\max\{q_{B}(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(k))\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\alpha(k)\in\binom{\bm{\beta}}{k},k\leq n,j\leq l\},

when the right hand side is nonzero, and the corresponding term grows at most like
\ln(|y|)
as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
when the left hand side is zero. In addition,
q_{u_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq q_{u}(n,l,\bm{\beta})-1
, and
q_{u_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq q_{u}(n,l,\bm{\beta})
, for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
.

Proof.

For 1), the proof essentially uses the same tools and a similar method to Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
, so we will only check this in the case for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})=(0,1,0)
and then comment on how the rest of the terms follow. Importantly, Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
only assumes existence and polynomial growth of derivatives of the solution
u
up to one order less than the derivative obtained there.

The result for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})=(0,0,0)
is just another way of writing Lemma
C.1
.

The differentiability and continuity of the derivatives is immediate via the explicit representation for
u

\displaystyle v(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{y}\frac{1}{a(x,\bar{y},\mu)\pi(\bar{y};x,\mu)}\biggl[\int_{-\infty}^{\bar{y}}B(x,\tilde{y},\mu)\pi(\tilde{y};x,\mu)d\tilde{y}\biggr]d\bar{y}

(76)

\displaystyle\pi(y;x,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{Z(x,\mu)}{a(x,y,\mu)}\exp\biggl(\int_{0}^{y}\frac{f(x,\bar{y},\mu)}{a(x,\bar{y},\mu)}d\bar{y}\biggr)

where
Z^{-1}(x,\mu)\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{1}{a(x,y,\mu)}\exp\biggl(\int_{0}^{y}\frac{f(x,\bar{y},\mu)}{a(x,\bar{y},\mu)}d\bar{y}\biggr)dy
is the normalizing constant.

To obtain the rate of polynomial growth of
u_{x}
, we differentiate the equation that
u
satisfies to get

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}u_{x}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=B_{x}(x,y,\mu)-f_{x}(x,y,\mu)u_{y}(x,y,\mu)-a_{x}(x,y,\mu)u_{yy}(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=B_{x}(x,y,\mu)-L^{(0,1,0)}_{x,\mu}u(x,y,\mu)

in the notation of Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
. But by the centering condition on
B
, we have that letting
B=h
in Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
,
u=v
in the statement of that same lemma. Thus we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(B_{x}(x,y,\mu)-L^{(0,1,0)}_{x,\mu}u(x,y,\mu)\right)\pi(dy;x,\mu)

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=0,

and the inhomogeneity of the elliptic PDE that
u_{x}
solves, in fact obeys the centering condition, and hence Lemma
C.1
applies. From the same lemma we already know that
q_{u,y}(0,0,0)=q_{B}(0,0,0)-1
and
q_{yy}=q_{B}(0,0,0)
. This establishes that
u_{x}
grows at most polynomially in
y
uniformly in
x,\mu
. Under the additional assumptions that
q_{f}(0,1,0)\leq 1
and
q_{a}(0,1,0)\leq 0
, we have the inhomogeneity is
O(|y|^{q_{B}(0,0,0)\vee q_{B}(0,1,0)})
. So by Lemma
C.1
,
q_{u,x}=q_{B}(0,0,0)\vee q_{B}(0,1,0),q_{u,x,y}=q_{B}(0,0,0)\vee q_{B}(0,1,0)-1,q_{u,x,y,y}=q_{B}(0,0,0)\vee q_{B}(0,1,0)
.

All of the bounds work in the same way, with the inhomogeneity of the elliptic PDE of the desired derivative of
u
solves being the integrand of the expression for the corresponding derivative of
\bar{B}(x,y,\mu)
from Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
. Put explicitly:

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]=D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}B(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},...,z_{n}]-

(77)

\displaystyle\hskip 113.81102pt-\sum_{k=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^{l}\sum_{\bm{p}_{k}}C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}L^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]D^{(n-k,l-j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k}))}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}^{\prime}_{n-k}}],

where the constants
C_{(\bm{p}_{k},j,n,l)}
are defined inductively in Remark A.3 in
[
5
]
and
L^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]
is the differential operator acting on
\phi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle L^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}_{x,\mu}[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi(y)=D^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}f(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime}(y)+D^{(k,j,\bm{\alpha}(\bm{p}_{k}))}a(x,y,\mu)[z_{\bm{p}_{k}}]\phi^{\prime\prime}(y).

The first
y
derivative of a lower order derivative in a parameter of
u
in the inhomogeneity is always multiplied by a derivative of
f
, and so if that derivative of
f
grows at most linearly in
y
, the growth of that term is at most that of that lower order derivative of
u
, and same for the second
y
derivative in a parameter of
u
in the inhomogeneity, which multiplied by a bounded lower order derivative of
a
. Thus it is clear the result follows by proceeding inductively on
n,l
.

The proof for 2) follows in the exact same way. We note here that Lemma A.2 in
[
5
]
holds for the linear functional derivatives
\bm{\delta}^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
in place of the Lions derivatives
D^{(n,l,\bm{\beta})}
if in addition we assume
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
v_{y},v_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}^{\prime}}(\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}))
.

The proof for 3) is similar to step 4 in the proof of Theorem 2.1 in
[
26
]
.
For the case
(n,l,\bm{\beta})=(0,0,0)
, we first note that

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu_{1}}[u(x,y,\mu_{1})-u(x,y,\mu_{2})]

\displaystyle=B(x,y,\mu_{1})-L_{x,\mu_{1}}u(x,y,\mu_{2})

\displaystyle=B(x,y,\mu_{1})-B(x,y,\mu_{2})-[L_{x,\mu_{1}}-L_{x,\mu_{2}}]u(x,y,\mu_{2}).

By the transfer formula in Lemma A.2 of
[
5
]
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(B(x,y,\mu_{1})-B(x,y,\mu_{2})-[L_{x,\mu_{1}}-L_{x,\mu_{2}}]u(x,y,\mu_{2})\right)\pi(dy,x,\mu_{1})=

\displaystyle\qquad=\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu_{1})\pi(dy;x,\mu_{1})-\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu_{2})\pi(dy;x,\mu_{2})=0,

so in fact the inhomogeneity in the above Poisson equation is centered. Now, rather than using Lemma
C.1
, we apply
[
72
]
Theorem 2 to get there is
k\in\mathbb{R}
sufficiently large and
C>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{|u(x,y,\mu_{1})-u(x,y,\mu_{2})|}{(1+|y|)^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq C\sup_{y\in\mathbb{R}}\frac{\biggl|B(x,y,\mu_{1})-B(x,y,\mu_{2})-[L_{x,\mu_{1}}-L_{x,\mu_{2}}]u(x,y,\mu_{2})\biggr|}{(1+|y|)^{k}}

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})

by the Lipschitz assumptions on
B,f,a
. Thus for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle|u(x,y,\mu_{1})-u(x,y,\mu_{2})|\leq C\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})(1+|y|)^{k}.

To see then that there are
k^{\prime},k^{\prime\prime}\in\mathbb{R},C^{\prime},C^{\prime\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle|u_{y}(x,y,\mu_{1})-u_{y}(x,y,\mu_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})(1+|y|)^{k^{\prime}}

\displaystyle|u_{yy}(x,y,\mu_{1})-u_{yy}(x,y,\mu_{2})|

\displaystyle\leq C\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})(1+|y|)^{k^{\prime\prime}},

we can apply the result of
[
68
]
Lemma B.1 and Remark B.2, and the last line of Proposition A.4 in the same reference.

The proof with
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
replaced by
x_{1},x_{2}
follows in the same way.

For the Lipschitz property in
z
, we first recall that for all
x,y,z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}D^{(1,0,0)}u(x,y,\mu)[z]=D^{(1,0,0)}B(x,y,\mu)[z]-L^{(1,0,0)}_{x,\mu}[z]u(x,y,\mu)

so

\displaystyle L_{x,\mu}\biggl[D^{(1,0,0)}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{1}]-D^{(1,0,0)}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{2}]\biggr]

\displaystyle=D^{(1,0,0)}B(x,y,\mu)[z_{1}]-L^{(1,0,0)}_{x,\mu}[z_{1}]u(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle-\biggl[D^{(1,0,0)}B(x,y,\mu)[z_{2}]-L^{(1,0,0)}_{x,\mu}[z_{2}]u(x,y,\mu)\biggr].

By the transfer formula in Lemma A.2 of
[
5
]
we have for all
x,z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}\left(D^{(1,0,0)}B(x,y,\mu)[z]-L^{(1,0,0)}_{x,\mu}[z]u(x,y,\mu)\right)\pi(dy;x,\mu)=D^{(0,1,0)}\int_{\mathbb{R}}B(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)[z]=0,

so the inhomogeneity in the Poisson equation above in centered. Thus, using the same argument as for the other Lipschitz continuity as well as the fact that
D^{(1,0,0)}B,D^{(1,0,0)}f,D^{(1,0,0)}a
are Lipschitz in
z
and
u_{y},u_{yy}
grow at most polynomially in
y
, we get there is
K\in\mathbb{R}
and
C>0
such that

\displaystyle\biggl|D^{(1,0,0)}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{1}]-D^{(1,0,0)}u(x,y,\mu)[z_{2}]\biggr|\leq C|z_{1}-z_{2}|(1+|y|)^{k},

and similarly for
D^{(1,0,0)}u_{y}
and
D^{(1,0,0)}u_{yy}
.

Then using the Poisson equation the derivatives satisfy given in Equation (
C.1
), we can iteratively use this same approach, along with the fact that products and sums of functions in
\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
remain in
\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, to achieve the full result.

∎

Lemma C.5
.

Suppose that for some complete collection of multi-indices
\bm{\zeta}
that
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. Then
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. If in addition,
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, then
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
. Further, if we have that
h,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, then
\int_{\mathbb{R}}h(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)\in\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\bm{\zeta}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
.

Proof.

This follows via Lemmas A.2 in
[
5
]
and
C.1
in a similar way to Lemma
C.4
. The details are omitted.

∎

C.2.
Result for the d-Dimensional Poisson Equation

Lemma C.6
.

Suppose
F\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d},G\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R},\tau\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d\times m}

\displaystyle|F(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-F(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})|+|G(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-G(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})|+\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-\tau(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq C[|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})],\forall x_{1},x_{2},y_{1},y_{2}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d}),

and there exists
\beta>0
such that for all
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
:

\displaystyle 2\langle F(x,y_{1},\mu)-F(x,y_{2},\mu),(y_{1}-y_{2})\rangle+3\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\tau(x,y_{1},\mu)-\tau(x,y_{2},\mu)\right\rVert}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\beta|y_{1}-y_{2}|^{2}.

Here
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is denoting the inner product on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\cdot\right\rVert}
the matrix norm. Also assume that
\tau
is bounded, and

\displaystyle|G(x,y,\mu)|,|F(x,y,\mu)|\leq C(1+|y|),\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Define the differential operator
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{x,\mu}
which for each
x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
acts on
\phi\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{x,\mu}\phi(y)

\displaystyle\coloneqq F(x,y,\mu)\cdot\nabla\phi(y)+\frac{1}{2}\tau\tau^{\top}(x,y,\mu)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\nabla^{2}\phi(y).

Then there is a unique invariant measure
\nu(\cdot;x,\mu)
associated to
\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{x,\mu}
for each
x,\mu
, and we assume the centering condition on
G
:

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}G(x,y,\mu)\nu(dy;x,\mu)

\displaystyle=0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

Finally, we assume the below derivatives all exist, are jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
and auxiliary variables where applicable, and satisfy:

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\{|\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{1})-\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{2})|,|\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{1})-\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{2})|\}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{1})-\partial^{2}_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{2})\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{1})-\partial^{2}_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{2})\right\rVert}\}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{x}\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{1})-\partial_{x}\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{2})\right\rVert}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{1})[\cdot]-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{2})[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}G(x,\mu,y_{1})[\cdot]-\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}G(x,\mu,y_{2})[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{1})[\cdot]-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y_{2})[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{1})[\cdot]-\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y_{2})[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu,y_{1})[\cdot,\cdot]-\partial^{2}_{\mu}G(x,\mu,y_{2})[\cdot,\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}

\displaystyle\leq C|y_{1}-y_{2}|

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\biggl\{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{y}\partial_{x}G(x,\mu,y)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{y}G(x,\mu,y)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}G(x,\mu,y)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq C

and same for
G
replaced by
F
and
\tau
, and in addition

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\biggl\{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{x}F(x,\mu,y)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{x}\tau(x,\mu,y)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}F(x,\mu,y)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\tau(x,\mu,y)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}F(x,\mu,y)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}\tau(x,\mu,y)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mu}F(x,\mu,y)[\cdot,\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})\otimes L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})},

\displaystyle\qquad\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mu}\tau(x,\mu,y)[\cdot,\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})\otimes L^{2}(\mu,\mathbb{R})}\biggr\}\leq C.

Then the partial differential equation

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathcal{L}}_{x,\mu}\chi(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=-G(x,y,\mu)

admits a unique classical solution
\chi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which has all of the above derivatives, and

\displaystyle\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\biggl\{|\chi(x,y,\mu)|,\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{x}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\chi(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{x}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\chi(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)},

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}\chi(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{\mu}\chi(x,y,\mu)[\cdot,\cdot]\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)\otimes L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}\biggr\}\leq C(1+|y|),\forall y\in\mathbb{R}^{2},

\displaystyle\sup_{x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}\max\biggl\{\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{y}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial^{2}_{y}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}\chi(x,y,\mu)\right\rVert}_{L^{2}(\mathbb{R},\mu)}\leq C.

Moreover, if all listed derivatives of
F,G,\tau
are jointly continuous in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
, then so are listed derivatives of
\chi
.

In the notation of Definition
2.4
, this conclusion reads
\chi\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
\chi_{y}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
\chi_{yy}\in\tilde{\mathcal{M}}^{(0,0,0)}_{p}(\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
with
\tilde{q}_{\chi}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}
,
\tilde{q}_{\chi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0,\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
, and
\tilde{q}_{\chi_{yy}}(0,0,0)\leq 0
where
\tilde{\bm{\zeta}},\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
are as in Equation (
27
).

Proof.

The arguments here follow closely those in
[
74
]
.
Existence and uniqueness for the invariant measure and strong solution from the Poisson equation are the subject of the beginning of Section 3.3 and Section 4.1 of
[
74
]
. The bound for
\chi
,
\partial_{y}\chi
,
\partial_{x}\chi,\partial_{\mu}\chi,\partial^{2}_{x}\chi
, and
\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\chi
is also the subject of Proposition 4.1/Section 6.3 of
[
74
]
, where we made the modification that
\tau,F
(their
g,f
respectively) are bounded in
x,\mu
, from which one can see that the bound on the solution is also uniform in
x,\mu
.

Thus we just need to show the bounds for
\partial^{2}_{y}\chi
,
\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\chi
,
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}\chi
,
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{x}\chi
, and
\partial^{2}_{\mu}\chi
. The bounds for
\partial^{2}_{y}\chi
,
\partial_{x}\partial_{y}\chi
and
\partial_{\mu}\partial_{y}\chi
are established in the recent
[
53
]
Proposition 3.1.

For the mixed partial derivative in
x
and
\mu
and the second partial derivative in
\mu
, we can follow the proof of Proposition 4.1 of
[
74
]
. The details are omitted here due to the similarity of the argument.
∎

C.3.
Some specific examples for which the assumptions of the paper hold

Proposition C.7
.

Suppose
A1)
-
A3)
hold. Let
\tilde{\bm{\zeta}},\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
be as in Equation (
27
), and consider also:

\displaystyle\bm{\zeta}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j_{1},0),(1,j_{2},j_{3}),(2,j_{4},(j_{5},0)),(3,0,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}=0,1,2,j_{2}+j_{3}\leq 2,j_{4}+j_{5}\leq 1\}

\displaystyle\bm{\zeta}_{1}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,2,0),(1,j_{1},j_{2}),(2,0,0)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}+j_{2}\leq 1\}.

In addition, suppose:

(1)

For
h=\tau_{1},\tau_{2},b
:

\displaystyle|h(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-h(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})|\leq C(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})),\forall x_{1},x_{2},y\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(2)

a,f,b\in\mathcal{M}^{\bm{\zeta}}_{p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
a_{y},f_{y},b_{y}\in\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}_{p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
,
a_{yy},f_{yy},b_{yy}\in\mathcal{M}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}_{p}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
, and
a_{yyy},f_{yyy},b_{yyy}
are bounded.

(3)

q_{a}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta},q_{a_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}},
and
q_{a_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
. In addition,
q_{a}(0,1,0)<0
.

(4)

q_{f}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta},q_{f}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}_{1},

q_{f_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}},
and
q_{f_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
. In addition,
q_{f}(0,1,0)<0
.

(5)

q_{b}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta},q_{b_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}},
and
q_{b_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
.

Then assumptions
A7)
and
A11)
hold.

Proof.

We first want to show the assumptions of Lemma
C.6
with
d=2,m=4
hold with
F_{1}(x,y,\mu)=f(x_{1},y_{1},\mu),F_{2}=f(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)
,
\tau_{11}(x,y,\mu)=\tau_{1}(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)
,
\tau_{12}(x,y,\mu)=\tau_{2}(x_{1},y_{1},\mu),\tau_{23}(x,y,\mu)=\tau_{1}(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)
,

\tau_{24}(x,y,\mu)=\tau_{2}(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)
, and
\tau_{ij}\equiv 0
otherwise, and
G(x,y,\mu)=b(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)[x_{1}]
or
G(x,y,\mu)=b(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)\Phi(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)
.

Under these assumptions we have
q_{\Phi}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{\Phi_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<0
and
q_{\Phi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<-1
for all
(n,l,\bf{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
via Lemma
C.4
.

The first Lipschitz assumption follows by (1) and
A2)
. The retraction to mean assumption is immediate from
A2)
. We also have
F
grows at most linearly in
|y|
by
A2)
, and
G
is in fact bounded by the above assumptions.

Checking the uniform Lipschitz in
y
assumptions for the derivatives of
G
, we have, for example, for the
x
derivative of the first choice, that:

\displaystyle b_{x}(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)[x_{1}]+b(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)[x_{1}]

and

\displaystyle b(x_{1},y_{1},\mu)\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{x}(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)[x_{1}]

need to be Lipschitz in
y
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
. To guarantee that the product of functions is Lipschitz without any more a priori information on the structure of each function, we must have that each function is Lipschitz and bounded. Since we make the assumptions that
q_{b}(0,j,0),q_{b_{y}}(0,j,0)\leq 0,j=0,1
and assumptions on
b,f,a
such that
q_{\Phi}(1,j_{1},j_{2}),q_{\Phi_{y}}(1,j_{1},j_{2})\leq 0,j_{1}+j_{2}\leq 1
, this assumption holds. This is where the requirement that for many
(n,l,\bm{\beta})
,
q_{b}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<0
is coming in to play.

If we differentiate
G
in the same way to see all the Lipschitz and bounded assumptions needed on each of
b
,
\Phi
, and
\partial_{\mu}\Phi
’s derivatives, we see that the only term that requires special care under these assumptions is
\partial_{\mu}\Phi_{yy}(x_{2},y_{2},\mu)[x_{1}]
. But using the equation elliptic equation that
\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x,y,\mu)[z]
satisfies for each
x,z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and Lemma
C.1
, we see for the second derivative of
\partial_{\mu}\Phi(x,y,\mu)[z]
to be uniformly Lipschitz in
y
, it is sufficient for

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}b(x,y,\mu)[z]-\partial_{\mu}f(x,y,\mu)[z]\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)-\partial_{\mu}a(x,y,\mu)[z]\Phi_{yy}(x,y,\mu)

to be uniformly Lipschitz in
y
.
\partial_{\mu}b(x,y,\mu)[z]
is already assumed to have this property, and
\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu),\Phi_{yy}(x,y,\mu)
are bounded and uniformly Lipschitz in
y
by assumption. Hence we just need in addition there that
q_{f}(1,0,0),q_{f_{y}}(1,0,0),q_{a}(1,0,0),q_{a_{y}}(1,0,0)\leq 0
as assumed.
