Clearly since we prove the Lipschitz property for each of the derivatives of
G
by ensuring each component is Lipschitz and bounded, the needed boundedness assumption for the mixed derivatives in
y
of
G
also holds.

Now to apply Lemma
C.6
, we just need to make sure that the needed Lipschitz and bounded assumptions on the derivatives of
F
and
\tau
hold. But these are implied by our assumptions on
f
and
a
.

Now we just need to improve the result of Lemma
C.6
to get
q_{\chi}(0,j,0)\leq 0,j=0,1
. We turn to
[
72
]
Theorem 2. We have
q_{G}(0,0,0)<0
, so
q_{\chi}(0,0,0)\leq 0
by a direct application of that Theorem. Then using that, as remarked in the proof of Lemma
C.2
, the transfer formula for the
x
derivatives in the
d
-dimensional case still hold in our setting and that
q_{\chi_{y}}(0,0,0),q_{\chi_{yy}}(0,0,0)\leq 0
by Lemma
C.6
and
q_{f}(0,1,0),q_{a}(0,1,0)<0
by assumption, we can get the inhomogeneity for the Poisson equation which
\partial_{x}\chi
satisfies also decays polynomially in
|y|
as
|y|\rightarrow\infty
uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R}^{2},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, so again by
[
72
]
Theorem 2,
q_{\chi}(0,1,0)\leq 0
.

Note that, while the sufficient conditions posed here for Assumption
A7)
automatically imply those for
A11)
, since Assumption
A11)
does not require specific polynomial growth, it can actually be proved under much weaker sufficient conditions - see Appendix A of
[
5
]
.
∎

Proposition C.8
.

Suppose the conditions of Proposition
C.7
and
A5)
hold. Let
\bm{\zeta}
and
\bm{\zeta}_{1}
be as in Proposition
C.7
, consider the collections of multi-indices from Equation (
27
), and let, in addition:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}

\displaystyle\ni\{(0,j_{1},0),(1,j_{2},j_{3}),(2,j_{4},(j_{5},j_{6})),(3,j_{7},(j_{8},0,0))

\displaystyle\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j_{1}\in\{0,1,...5\},j_{3}\leq 4,j_{2}+j_{3}\leq 5,j_{5}+j_{6}\leq 2,j_{4}+j_{5}+j_{6}\leq 3,j_{7}+j_{8}\leq 1\}.

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}

\displaystyle=\bar{\bm{\zeta}}\cup\bar{\bm{\zeta}}_{w+2}

\displaystyle\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}

\displaystyle\ni\{(j,j_{2},0),(1,j_{2},j_{3})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}j=0,1,2,j_{2}=0,1,j_{3}=1,...,w+2\}.

In addition, suppose:

(1)

For
h=\sigma,g,c
:

\displaystyle|h(x_{1},y_{1},\mu_{1})-h(x_{2},y_{2},\mu_{2})|\leq C(|x_{1}-x_{2}|+|y_{1}-y_{2}|+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})),\forall x_{1},x_{2},y\in\mathbb{R},\mu_{1},\mu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}).

(2)

g,\sigma,\tau_{1},c\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}
and
f,a,b\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}

(3)

q_{a}(0,3,0)\leq 0
,
q_{f}(0,3,0)\leq 1
,
q_{b}(0,3,0)\leq 3

(4)

q_{\sigma}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{c}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1,q_{g}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{\tau_{1}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2
for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
and
q_{\sigma}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{c}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2,q_{g}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{\tau_{1}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 3
for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}
.

(5)

b,f,\tau_{1},\tau_{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}}
and
c,\sigma,g\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}}
.

(6)

b,f,a\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+3}},\sigma,\tau_{1},c,g\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+2}}
, and

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}b(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}b_{x}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}f(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}f_{x}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}a(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}a_{x}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}g(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\sigma(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}c(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2}

\displaystyle\leq C(1+|y|^{k}),

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}.

(7)

There exists
\bar{\lambda}_{-}>0
such that
\bar{D}(x,\mu)\geq\bar{\lambda}_{-}
for all
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}
.

Then assumptions
A4)
and
A6)
-
A13)
, hold, and
A’13)
holds if we replace
w
with
r
in (6).

Proof.

A4)
follows from the above Lipschitz properties, writing the system of SDEs (
1
) in terms of the empirical projections of the coefficients and using standard strong existence and uniqueness results (see Proposition A.1 in
[
7
]
) for the weakly interacting system (
1
) and applying Theorem 2.1 in
[
34
]
to the IID McKean-Vlasov system (
57
).

For the limiting system (
25
), we have, noting that
\hat{\bm{\zeta}}
is the same
\hat{\bm{\zeta}}
from assumption
A10)
, and
\hat{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
is just
\hat{\bm{\zeta}}
with one extra
x
derivative in all spacial components, that by Lemma
C.4
,
\Phi,\Phi_{x},\Phi_{y},\Phi_{xy}\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}
, and thus under these assumptions
\gamma,D\in\mathcal{M}_{p,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}
, and hence by Lemma
C.5

\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}\in\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}
. Using that
x\mapsto\sqrt{x}
is smooth with bounded derivatives of all orders on bounded sets in
[\bar{\lambda}_{-},+\infty)
, we get via chain rule that in fact
\bar{D}^{1/2}\in\mathcal{M}_{b,L}^{\hat{\bm{\zeta}}}
. This immediately implies
A10)
, and also yields by definition that
\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}
are bounded and Lipschitz in
x,\mathbb{W}_{2}
. So again Theorem 2.1 in
[
34
]
applies, and we gain strong existence and uniqueness of the averaged McKean-Vlasov SDE (
25
). Note that this is the only place where an assumption on the limiting coefficients, that is (7), is being used, and as per Remark
4.4
this assumption holds in all but pathological cases.

For assumption
A6)
, we already showed from the assumptions in Proposition
C.7
,
q_{\Phi}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{\Phi_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<0
and
q_{\Phi_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<-1
for all
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\bm{\zeta}
, which is much stronger than what we require.

For
A7)
and
A11)
, we already showed the result in Proposition
C.7
.

For
A8)
, we can check that for each of the 3 choices of
F
, that
q_{F},q_{F}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1
for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
and
q_{F}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2
for
(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}
, so the result follows by Lemma
C.4
. In particular, since one of our choices of
F
is
\gamma
, which involves
\Phi_{x}
, we use that since
q_{b}(0,3,0)\leq 3,q_{f}(0,3,0)\leq 1,q_{a}(0,3,0)\leq 0
, Lemma
C.4
implies
q_{\Phi_{y}}(0,3,0)\leq 2
.

For
A9)
, we use that all the terms in the products involved in each of the functions are bounded and jointly Lipschitz. The Lipschitz properties in
x,y
can be extrapolated from boundedness of each of the respective derivatives of the functions and in
\mathbb{W}_{2}
follow from the boundedness of the Lions derivatives by
[
9
]
Remark 5.27.

For
A12)
, we have via Lemma
C.4
that
\Phi,\Phi_{x},\Phi_{y},\Phi_{xy}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}}
(by construction of
\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
), so that in fact all the listed functions are in
\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\mathring{\bm{\zeta}}}
. By Lemma
C.5
, this also implies the continuity for the Linear Functional Derivatives in
A13)
/
A’13)
by definition.

And finally, for
A13)
/
A’13)
, we have via Lemma
C.4
that
\Phi,\Phi_{x},\Phi_{y},\Phi_{xy}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+2}}
(by construction of
\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+3}
). Then in fact
\gamma,D\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+2}}
, and by Lemma
C.5
, we get
\bar{\gamma},\bar{D}\in\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,w+2}}
.

For the regularity of the linear functional derivatives, via the equality (
75
) given by Lemma
C.2
, we see it is sufficient to show

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\Phi(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\Phi_{y}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\Phi_{x}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\Phi_{xy}(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{w+2}\leq C(1+|y|^{k}),

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}.
This follows as in the proof of the Lipschitz property in
C.4
, iteratively using the equation (
C.1
) and that the coefficient for the growth rate in
y
can be written in terms of the inhomogeneity via
[
72
]
Theorem 2 and the assumption (6), then applying
[
68
]
Lemma B1 / Remark B2 to get the result for the derivatives in
y
as well.
∎

The examples that follow, examples
C.9
-
C.11
, present concrete cases covered by our assumptions.

Example C.9
.

(A case with full dependence of the coefficients on
(x,y,\mu)
)
Suppose
\tau_{1},\tau_{2},\sigma>0
are constant with
\sigma
large enough that
\bar{D}(x,\mu)>0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, where
\bar{D}
is as in Equation (
24
), and the other coefficients take the form

\displaystyle b(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=q\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)p_{b}(x),\quad c(x,y,\mu)=r_{c}(y)+p_{c}(x)+\langle\mu,\phi_{c}\rangle

\displaystyle f(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=-\kappa y+\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle,\quad g(x,y,\mu)=r_{g}(y)+p_{g}(x)+\langle\mu,\phi_{g}\rangle,

where here
\kappa>0
and
\langle\phi,\mu\rangle\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}}\phi(z)\mu(dz)
. Suppose also that
q\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
is odd, there is
\beta>0
such that
|q(z)|,|q^{\prime}(z)|,|q^{\prime\prime}(z)|,|q^{\prime\prime\prime}(z)|\leq C(1+|z|)^{-\beta}
,
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert r_{c}^{\prime}\right\rVert}_{\infty}\leq C
,
|r_{g}|_{C_{b}^{1}(\mathbb{R})}\leq C
,
\phi_{c},\phi_{g},\phi_{f}\in\mathcal{S}_{w+2}
, and
p_{c},p_{g}\in C_{b}^{w+2}(\mathbb{R}),p_{b}\in C_{b}^{w+3}(\mathbb{R})
. Then assumptions
\ref{assumption:uniformellipticity}-\ref{assumption:limitingcoefficientsregularity}
hold, and
A’13)
holds if
w
is replaced by
r
.

Proof.

A1)
and
A5)
are immediate.
A2)
follows from noting that
\eta(x,y,\mu)=\eta(\mu)=\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle
, so
\partial_{\mu}\eta(\mu)[z]=\phi^{\prime}_{f}(z)
(see Example 1 in Section 5.2.2 in
[
9
]
) and by Remark 5.27 in
[
9
]
,
|\eta(\mu_{1})-\eta(\mu_{2})|\leq\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi_{f}^{\prime}\right\rVert}_{\infty}\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}).
In addition,

\displaystyle 2(f(x,y_{1},\mu)-f(x,y_{2},\mu))(y_{1}-y_{2})+3|\tau_{1}(x,y_{1},\mu)-\tau_{1}(x,y_{2},\mu)|^{2}+3|\tau_{2}(x,y_{1},\mu)-\tau_{2}(x,y_{2},\mu)|^{2}

\displaystyle=-2\kappa(y_{1}-y_{2})^{2}.

For
A3)
, we can find via the explicit form of
\pi
in Lemma
C.2
(or the fact that the frozen process is given by the Vasicek model and hence the transition density is an explicitly computable Gaussian) that

\displaystyle\pi(y;\mu)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi a}}\exp\biggl(-\frac{\kappa}{2a}[y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle]^{2}\biggr),

so

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}}b(x,y,\mu)\pi(dy;x,\mu)

\displaystyle=p_{b}(x)\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)\exp\biggl(-\frac{\kappa}{2a}[y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle]^{2}\biggr)dy

\displaystyle=p_{b}(x)\sqrt{\frac{k}{2\pi a}}\int_{\mathbb{R}}q(y)\exp\biggl(-\frac{\kappa}{2a}y^{2}\biggr)dy

\displaystyle=0,\forall x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})

since the integrand is odd.

For the rest of the assumptions, we use Propositions
C.7
and
C.8
. We have

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{j+k}}{\partial y^{j}\partial x^{k}}b(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=q^{(j)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)p^{(k)}_{b}(x)

\displaystyle\partial^{l}_{\mu}\frac{\partial^{j+k}}{\partial y^{j}\partial x^{k}}b(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{l}]

\displaystyle=q^{(j+l)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)p^{(k)}_{b}(x)(-\kappa^{-1})^{l}\prod_{m=1}^{l}\phi^{\prime}(z_{m})

\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{l}}{\delta m^{l}}\frac{\partial^{j+k}}{\partial y^{j}\partial x^{k}}b(x,y,\mu)[z_{1},z_{2},...,z_{l}]

\displaystyle=q^{(j+l)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)p^{(k)}_{b}(x)(-\kappa^{-1})^{l}\prod_{m=1}^{l}\phi(z_{m})

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\frac{\partial^{j+k}}{\partial y^{j}\partial x^{k}}b(x,y,\mu)[z]

\displaystyle=q^{(j+1)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)p^{(k)}_{b}(x)\phi^{(l)}_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}f(x,y,\mu)[z]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}_{f}(z),\quad\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}f(x,y,\mu)[z]=\phi^{(l)}_{f}(z)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial^{j}}{\partial y^{j}}h(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=r^{(j)}_{h}(y),\quad\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x^{k}}h(x,y,\mu)=p^{(j)}_{h}(x)

\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}h(x,y,\mu)[z]

\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}_{h}(z),\quad\frac{\partial^{l}}{\partial z^{l}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}h(x,y,\mu)[z]=\phi^{(l)}_{h}(z)

for
h=c,g
and
j,k,l\in\mathbb{N}
such that the above derivatives are defined.

From this we can see that
b_{y},b_{x},\partial_{\mu}b(x,y,\mu)[z]
are all uniformly bounded, and hence (1) in Proposition
C.7
holds.

For (2)-(5),
a
is constant, and all the considered derivatives of
f
are uniformly
0
except for
f_{y}=-\kappa
,
\partial^{l}_{z}\partial_{\mu}f(x,y,\mu)[z]=\phi^{(1+l)}_{f}(z),l=0,1,2
, all of which are uniformly bounded in
y
. All the involved derivatives of
b
are seen to be bounded functions of
x,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}
multiplied by
q^{(j)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)
for
j\in\{0,1,2,3\}
, so since the translation is uniformly bounded in
\mu
, we see all of the listed
q_{b}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{b_{y}}(n,l,\bm{\beta}),q_{b_{yy}}(n,l,\bm{\beta})<0
. So the assumptions of
C.7
hold.

Now turning to Proposition
C.8
, we have
h_{y},h_{x},\partial_{\mu}h(x,y,\mu)[z]
,
h=g,c
are all uniformly bounded, and hence (1) holds.

(2) follows from observing that the desired derivatives in
x
of
f
and of
c,g
are independent of
\mu
and bounded Lipschitz in
x
.
\partial_{z}^{l}\partial_{\mu}p
for
p=f,c,g
,
l=0,...,4
only depends on
z
, and is Lipschitz for all
l
. All the listed derivatives of
b
can easily be shown to be Lipschitz in
x,z
uniformly in
y,\mu
via the representations above, and since they take the form of bounded functions in
x,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3},z_{4}
multiplied by
q^{(j)}\biggl(y-\frac{1}{\kappa}\langle\mu,\phi_{f}\rangle\biggr)
for
j\in\{0,1,2,3,4\}
, of which the Lions derivative is uniformly bounded, we have by Remark 5.27 in
[
9
]
that they are Lipschitz in
\mathbb{W}_{2}
uniformly in
x,y,z
.

q_{b}(0,3,0)<0
, so (3) holds.

The first and second derivatives of
c,g
in
x
are bounded, their first derivative in
\mu
is bounded and its derivative in
z
are bounded, and the rest of the derivatives of (4) are
0
.

For (5)-(6)
f,c,g
, the listed derivatives do not depend on
y
or
\mu
, and are uniformly bounded, with the linear functional derivatives in (6) being in
\mathcal{S}_{w+2}
by assumption. For
b
, all the derivatives in
\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{3}
are bounded by
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert q\right\rVert}_{\infty}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert p_{b}\right\rVert}_{C_{b}^{w+3}}
, the second linear functional derivatives are uniformly bounded by their representation above, and
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\partial^{k}}{\partial x}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}b(x,y,\mu)[z]\right\rVert}_{w+2}\leq\mathinner{\!\left\lVert q^{\prime}\right\rVert}_{\infty}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert p_{b}\right\rVert}_{C^{k}_{b}(\mathbb{R})}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi_{f}\right\rVert}_{w+2}\leq C,k=0,1
.

Finally, (7) holds by supposition (noting that by the form provided for
\bar{D}
in Equation (
26
) and the fact that
\Phi
does not depend on
\sigma
that such a sufficiently large choice exists).

∎

Example C.10
.

(A case where
\Phi
and
\pi
are independent of
x,\mu
)
Consider the case:

\displaystyle b(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=b(y),\quad c(x,y,\mu)=c_{1}(x)+\langle\mu,c_{2}(x-\cdot)\rangle,\quad\sigma(x,y,\mu)\equiv\sigma

\displaystyle f(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle=-\kappa y+\eta(y),\quad g(x,y,\mu)=g_{1}(x)+\langle\mu,g_{2}(x-\cdot)\rangle,\quad\tau_{1}(x,y,\mu)\equiv\tau_{1},\quad\tau_{2}(x,y,\mu)\equiv\tau_{2}.

Suppose
\eta\in C^{1}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
with
\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\eta^{\prime}\right\rVert}_{\infty}<\kappa
,
c_{1},g_{1},c_{2},g_{2}\in C_{b}^{w+2}(\mathbb{R})
,
c_{2},g_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{w+2}
,
\tau_{1}^{2}+\tau_{2}^{2}>0
, and
b
is Lipschitz continuous,
O(|y|^{1/2})
, and satisfies the centering condition (
21
). Then Assumptions
A1)
-
A13)
hold. Furthermore, if this holds with
w
replaced by
r
, then Assumption
A’13)
holds.

Proof.

Note that here
\Phi
does not depend on
x
or
\mu
, there is no need for Lemma
6.3
, which adds to the simplification of things (we don’t need to check Assumption
A7)
. In particular, there is no need for the extremely restrictive assumptions needed to apply Lemma
C.6
since, as we will see, an application of Proposition A.2 from
[
68
]
is sufficient to handle Assumption
A11)
, and the rest of the Poisson Equations are 1-dimensional.

Assumptions
A1)
,
A2)
,
A3)
, and
A5)
are immediate.

For
A4)
, we have for
F(x,\mu)=c(x,\mu)
or
g(x,\mu)

F_{x}(x,\mu)=F_{1}^{\prime}(x)+\langle\mu,F^{\prime}(x-\cdot)\rangle
and
\partial_{\mu}F(x,\mu)[z]=-F_{2}^{\prime}(x-z)
are bounded, so for all coefficients joint Lipschitz continuity in
(x,y,\mathbb{W}_{2})
holds (using again Example 1 in Section 5.2.2 and Remark 5.27 in
[
9
]
), and the result holds in the same way as in Proposition
C.8
.

For
A6)
, we just need
\Phi(y)
grows at most linearly in
y
and
\Phi^{\prime}(y)
is bounded. From Lemma
C.1
, we have in fact
\Phi
is
O(|y|^{1/2})
and
\Phi^{\prime}
is
O(|y|^{-1/2})
.

For
A8)
, we have
\gamma(x,y,\mu)=[g_{1}(x)+\langle\mu,g_{2}(x-\cdot)\rangle]\Phi^{\prime}(y)+c_{1}(x)+\langle\mu,c_{2}(x-\cdot)\rangle
and
D(x,y,\mu)=D(y)=b(y)\Phi(y)+\sigma\tau_{1}\Phi^{\prime}(y)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}
. Then for the case
F=\gamma
,
\Xi(x,y,\mu)=\tilde{\Xi}(y)[g_{1}(x)+\langle\mu,g_{2}(x-\cdot)\rangle]
where

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\tilde{\Xi}(y)=\Phi^{\prime}(y)-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi^{\prime}(y)\pi(dy).

\Phi^{\prime}
is
O(|y|^{-1/2})
, so by Lemma
C.1
,
\tilde{\Xi}\in C^{2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
. Using
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
have two bounded derivatives, it is plain to see the result holds. A similar proof shows the result holds with
F=\sigma\psi_{1}(t,x,y)+[\tau_{1}\psi_{1}(t,x,y)+\tau_{2}\psi_{2}(t,x,y)]\Phi^{\prime}(y),\psi_{1},\psi_{2}\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
. Since
\Phi
and
b
are
O(|y|^{1/2})
,
D
is
O(|y|)
, so by Lemma
C.1

\Xi(y)
corresponding to
F(y)=D(y)
is
O(|y|)
, with
\Xi^{\prime}
bounded.

For
A9)
, we use the Lipschitz and boundedness properties of
\Phi^{\prime}(y)
from Lemma
C.1
. The result then follows by the previously noted Lipschitz properties of
c
and
g
, and hence
\gamma
.

For
A10)
,
\bar{D}
is constant and
\bar{\gamma}(x,\mu)=\alpha g(x,\mu)+c(x,\mu)
, for
\alpha=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi^{\prime}(y)\pi(dy)
. The result thus follows from
g_{1},c_{1}\in C^{5}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and
g_{2},c_{2}\in C^{6}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
.

For
A11)
, we have
\tilde{\chi}(x,y,\mu)=\tilde{\chi}(y)
grows linearly in
y
and
\tilde{\chi}^{\prime}(y)
is
O(|y|^{3})
via Proposition A.2 of
[
68
]
.

For
A12)
, none of the listed functions depend on
\mu
other than
\gamma
, and
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\gamma(x,y,\mu)[z]=g_{2}(x-z)\Phi^{\prime}(y)+c_{2}(x-z)
is bounded.

For
A13)
,
\bar{D}
is constant and
\bar{\gamma}(x,\mu)=\alpha g(x,\mu)+c(x,\mu)
, for
\alpha=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Phi^{\prime}(y)\pi(dy)
, so the result follows from
g_{1},g_{2},c_{1},c_{2}\in C^{w+2}_{b}(\mathbb{R})
, and
g_{2},c_{2}\in\mathcal{S}_{w+2}.
The proof for extending to
A’13)
follows in the same way, replacing
w
by
r
.

∎

Example C.11
.

(The case without full-coupling)
Consider the case where

\displaystyle b(x,y,\mu)

\displaystyle\equiv 0,\quad\sigma(x,y,\mu)=\sigma(x,\mu).

In this setting, it is known that when also
g\equiv 0
and
\tau_{1}\equiv 0
, under sufficient conditions on
c,\sigma,f
and
\tau_{2}
, we can expect not only convergence in distribution of
\bar{X}^{\epsilon}\overset{d}{=}\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}
from Equation (
57
) to
X
from Equation (
25
), but also convergence in
L^{2}
. It is easily seen that this also holds when
g,\tau_{1}\neq 0
if they are sufficiently regular.

Note that in the limiting coefficients from Equation (
24
), we have
\Phi\equiv 0
, so
\bar{\gamma}(x,\mu)=\bar{c}(x,\mu)
and
\bar{D}(x,\mu)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(x,\mu)
. In this setting, we can see immediately that there is no need for Assumptions
A6)
,
A7)
, and
A11)
.
A8)
need only hold with
F=c
and
F=\psi\in C^{\infty}_{c}([0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R})
. We will see that, since we can gain the aforementioned
L^{2}
averaging, there is no need for Theorem
7.2
, and hence for Assumption
A10)
.

Sufficient conditions for Theorem
3.2
to hold in this case are:
A1)
-
A3)
,
A5)
,
A9)
,
A12)
, and

(1)

c,a,f\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
with
q_{f}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1
,
q_{a}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 0
,
q_{c}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 2
,
\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}
and
q_{c}(n,l,\bm{\beta})\leq 1
,
\forall(n,l,\bm{\beta})\in\tilde{\bm{\zeta}}_{1}
.

(2)

\sigma^{2}\in\mathcal{M}_{b}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,r+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))\cap\mathcal{M}_{\bm{\delta},b}^{\bar{\bm{\zeta}}_{r+2}}(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}))
and

\sup_{x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}(R)}\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\sigma^{2}(x,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{r+2}<\infty.

(3)

f,a,c\in\mathcal{M}_{p}^{\bm{\zeta}_{x,r+2}}
and

\displaystyle\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}f(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{r+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}a(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{r+2},\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\frac{\delta}{\delta m}c(x,y,\mu)[\cdot]\right\rVert}_{r+2}\leq C(1+|y|^{k}),

uniformly in
x\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
for some
k\in\mathbb{N}.

In the above the referenced collections of multi-indices are from Equation (
27
).

Proof.

A8)
holds with
F=c
and
\psi
via an application of Lemmas
C.4
and
C.5
, using (1).

Further, Lemma
C.5
gives
\bar{c}(x,\mu)
is Lipschitz continuous, so
A4)
holds in the same way as Examples
C.9
and
C.10
via the Lipschitz properties imposed on
c,\sigma,f,\tau_{2}
from assumptions
A2)
and
A9)
.

Since
A8)
holds with
F=c
, one can see that Proposition 4.5 of
[
5
]
holds with
F=c
and
\psi=1
(noting that under these assumptions the norm may be moved inside the expectation with little change to the proof method). Then:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}-X_{t}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl\{\mathbb{E}[\biggl|\int_{0}^{t}c(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},Y^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\bar{c}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))ds\biggr|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}|\sigma(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\sigma(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|^{2}ds]\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl\{\mathbb{E}[\biggl|\int_{0}^{t}c(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},Y^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\bar{c}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))ds\biggr|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}|\bar{c}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\bar{c}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|^{2}ds]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}|\sigma(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\sigma(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|^{2}ds]\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq C\biggl\{\mathbb{E}[\biggl|\int_{0}^{t}c(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},Y^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\bar{c}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))ds\biggr|^{2}]+\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}|\bar{c}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\bar{c}(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|^{2}ds]

\displaystyle+\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{t}|\sigma(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}))-\sigma(X_{s},\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))|^{2}ds]\biggr\}

\displaystyle\leq C\epsilon^{2}(1+t^{2}+t)+C\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}-X_{s}|^{2}]+\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{s}),\mathcal{L}(X_{s}))ds

where in the last step we used Proposition 4.5 of
[
5
]
with
F=c
and
\psi=1
, the assumed Lipschitz continuity of
\sigma
, and the inherited Lipschitz continuity of
\bar{c}
via Lemma
C.5
. Bounding the 2-Wasserstein distance between the Laws by the difference in squared expectation of the processes, we get by Grönwall’s inequality:

\displaystyle\sup_{t\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}-X_{t}|^{2}]

\displaystyle\leq C(T)\epsilon^{2}.

Thus, in the proof of Lemma
7.6
, we can circumnavigate using Theorem
7.2
in the last line, and instead use

\displaystyle a^{2}(N)N\biggl|\mathbb{E}[\phi(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}]-\mathbb{E}[\phi(X_{t})]\biggr|^{2}+4a^{2}(N)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}(N)NC(T)\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}-X_{t}|^{2}]\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi^{\prime}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}+2a(N)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{\infty}

\displaystyle\leq a^{2}(N)N\epsilon^{2}C(T)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi^{\prime}\right\rVert}^{2}_{\infty}+2a(N)\mathinner{\!\left\lVert\phi\right\rVert}_{\infty}.

This shows we can circumnavigate the need for Assumption
A10)
, and also shows that the bound from Lemma
7.6
can be improved to
C(T)|\phi|_{1}^{2}
.

Lastly, Assumption
A’13)
holds immediately for
\bar{D}=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}
using (2), and can be seen to hold for
\bar{\gamma}=\bar{c}
using (3) and the same proof as in Proposition
C.8
.
∎

Remark C.12
.

One can in fact see in the setting of Example
C.11
that, as noted, the bound in Lemma
7.6
can be improved to
C(T)|\phi|_{1}^{2}
, and further, that since
R_{5}^{i}
in Lemma
7.7
is zero, the bound on
R^{N}_{t}(\phi)
in the same Lemma can be improved to
\bar{R}(N,T)|\phi|_{3}
. Moreover, via the bound above, we can see via triangle inequality and Lemma
7.5
that
\sup_{s\in[0,T]}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\biggl|\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}_{s}-X^{i}_{s}\biggr|^{2}\biggr]\leq C(T)[\epsilon^{2}+\frac{1}{N}+\frac{1}{Na^{2}(N)}],
where
\{X^{i}\}_{i=1}^{N}
are IID copies of the limiting McKean-Vlasov Equation (
25
) driven by the same Brownian motions as the
\tilde{X}^{i,\epsilon,N}
’s. This allows for the proof of the Laplace Principle Upper Bound in Proposition
10.1
to go through in the same way as in Subsection 4.4 of
[
8
]
, and eliminates the need for the approximation argument therein. Thus, in fact, the rate function can be posed on
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-\rho})
and taken to be infinite outside of
C([0,T];\mathcal{S}_{-v})
as in Corollary
3.4
. This also allows us to see that (2) and (3) in Example
C.11
can be relaxed by replacing
r
with
\rho
.

Appendix D
On Differentiation of Functions on Spaces of Measures

We will need the following two definitions from
[
9
]
:

Definition D.1
.

Given a function
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, we may define a lifting of
u
to
\tilde{u}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
via
\tilde{u}(X)=u(\mathcal{L}(X))
for
X\in L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
. We assume
\tilde{\Omega}
is a Polish space,
\tilde{\mathcal{F}}
its Borel
\sigma
-field, and
\tilde{\mathbb{P}}
is an atomless probability measure (since
\tilde{\Omega}
is Polish, this is equivalent to every singleton having zero measure).

Here, denoting by
\mu(|\cdot|^{r})\coloneqq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{r}\mu(dx)
for
r>0
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\coloneqq\{\mu\in\mathcal{P}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mu(|\cdot|^{2})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x|^{2}\mu(dx)<\infty\}.

\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
is a Polish space under the
L^{2}
-Wasserstein distance

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})\coloneqq\inf_{\pi\in\mathcal{C}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}}\biggl[\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\times\mathbb{R}^{d}}|x-y|^{2}\pi(dx,dy)\biggr]^{1/2},

where
\mathcal{C}_{\mu_{1},\mu_{2}}
denotes the set of all couplings of
\mu_{1},\mu_{2}
.

We say
u
is
L-differentiable
or
Lions-differentiable
at
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
if there exists a random variable
X_{0}
on some
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
satisfying the above assumptions,
\mathcal{L}(X_{0})=\mu_{0}
and
\tilde{u}
is Fréchet differentiable at
X_{0}
.

The Fréchet derivative of
\tilde{u}
can be viewed as an element of
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
by identifying
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R}^{d})
and its dual. From this, one can find that if
u
is L-differentiable at
\mu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})
, there is a deterministic measurable function
\xi\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
such that
D\tilde{u}(X_{0})=\xi(X_{0})
, and that
\xi
is uniquely defined
\mu_{0}
-almost everywhere on
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. We denote this equivalence class of
\xi\in L^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d},\mu_{0};\mathbb{R}^{d})
by
\partial_{\mu}u(\mu_{0})
and call
\partial_{\mu}u(\mu_{0})[\cdot]\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{d}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
the
Lions derivative
of
u
at
\mu_{0}
. Note that this definition is independent of the choice of
X_{0}
and
(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}})
. See
[
9
]
Section 5.2.

To avoid confusion when
u
depends on more variables than just
\mu
, if
\partial_{\mu}u(\mu_{0})
is differentiable at
z_{0}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we denote its derivative at
z_{0}
by
\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}u(\mu_{0})[z_{0}]
.

Definition D.2
.

(
[
9
]
Definition 5.83) We say
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is
Fully

\mathbf{C^{2}}
if the following conditions are satisfied:

(1)

u
is
C^{1}
in the sense of L-differentiation, and its first derivative has a jointly continuous version
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z)\mapsto\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)[z]\in\mathbb{R}
.

(2)

For each fixed
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the version of
\mathbb{R}\ni z\mapsto\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)[z]\in\mathbb{R}
from the first condition is differentiable on
\mathbb{R}
in the classical sense and its derivative is given by a jointly continuous function
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z)\mapsto\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)[z]\in\mathbb{R}
.

(3)

For each fixed
z\in\mathbb{R}
, the version of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni\mu\mapsto\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)[z]\in\mathbb{R}
in the first condition is continuously L-differentiable component-by-component, with a derivative given by a function
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z,\bar{z})\mapsto\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z][\bar{z}]\in\mathbb{R}
such that for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
X\in L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R})
with
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu
,
\partial^{2}u(\mu)[z][X]
gives the Fréchet derivative at
X
of
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R})\ni X^{\prime}\mapsto\partial_{\mu}u(\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime}))[z]
for every
z\in\mathbb{R}
. Denoting
\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z][\bar{z}]
by
\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]
, the map
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z,\bar{z})\mapsto\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]
is also assumed to be continuous in the product topology.

Remark D.3
.

In this paper we will in fact also look at functions
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
which are required to have
3
Lions Derivatives. We will assume such functions are
Fully

\mathbf{C^{2}}
, and satisfy:

(4)

For each each fixed
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
the version of
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}
in Definition
D.2
(3) is differentiable on
\mathbb{R}^{2}
in the classical sense and its derivative is given by a jointly continuous function
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z_{1},z_{2})\mapsto\nabla_{z}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]=(\partial_{z_{1}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}],\partial_{z_{2}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}])\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

(5)

For each fixed
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
, the version of
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\ni\mu\mapsto\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}
in Definition
D.2
(3) is continuously L-differentiable component-by-component, with a derivative given by a function
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\partial^{3}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}][z_{3}]\in\mathbb{R}
such that for any
\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
and
X\in L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R})
with
\mathcal{L}(X)=\mu
,
\partial^{3}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}][X]
gives the Fréchet derivative at
X
of
L^{2}(\tilde{\Omega},\tilde{\mathcal{F}},\tilde{\mathbb{P}};\mathbb{R})\ni X^{\prime}\mapsto\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mathcal{L}(X^{\prime}))[z_{1},z_{2}]
for every
(z_{1},z_{2})\in\mathbb{R}^{2}
. Denoting
\partial^{3}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}][z_{3}]
by
\partial^{2}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}]
, the map
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\ni(\mu,z_{1},z_{2},z_{3})\mapsto\partial^{3}_{\mu}u(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2},z_{3}]
is also assumed to be continuous in the product topology.

Though we don’t require higher than 3 Lions derivatives in this paper, when we state general results for higher Lions derivatives in terms of the spaces from Definition
2.4
, we assume the analogous higher continuity.

We will also make use of another notion of differentiation of functions of probability measures: the linear functional derivative.

Definition D.4
.

(
[
9
]
Definition 5.43) Let
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We say
p
has
Linear Functional Derivative

\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p
if there exists a function
(z,\mu)\ni\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mapsto\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p(\mu)[z]\in\mathbb{R}
continuous in the product topology on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
such that for any bounded subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the function
\mathbb{R}\ni z\mapsto\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p(\mu)[z]
is of at most quadratic growth uniformly in
\mu
for
\mu\in\mathcal{K}
, and for all
\nu_{1},\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}

\displaystyle p(\nu_{2})-p(\nu_{1})=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p((1-r)\nu_{1}+r\nu_{2})[z](\nu_{2}(dz)-\nu_{1}(dz))dr.

Note in particular that this implies that
p
is continuous on
\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

The second linear functional derivative is said to exist if the linear functional derivative of
\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p(\mu)[z_{1}]
as defined above exists for each
z_{1}\in\mathbb{R}
. For any bounded subset
\mathcal{K}\subset\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, the function
(z_{1},z_{2})\ni\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\mapsto\frac{\delta}{\delta m}\biggl(\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p(\mu)[z_{1}]\biggr)[z_{2}]\coloneqq\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}p(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}
, is of at most quadratic growth uniformly in
\mu
for
\mu\in\mathcal{K}
,
(z_{1},z_{2},\mu)\ni\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\mapsto\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}p(\mu)[z_{1},z_{2}]\in\mathbb{R}
and is assumed to be continuous in the product topology on
\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
.

Remark D.5
.

See Section 5.4.1 of
[
9
]
for well-posedness of the above notion of differentiability and relation to Lions derivative. In particular, under sufficient regularity on
u\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
,
\partial_{\mu}u(\mu)[z]=\partial_{z}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}u(\mu)[z]
. For a formal understanding of the linear functional derivative as a Fréchet Derivative, see p.21 of
[
10
]
. Lastly, it is important to note that the linear functional derivative is only defined up to a constant by definition. This is usually not of importance, at it normally arises when studying fluctuations of measures. In particular, applying
\tilde{Z}^{N}_{t}
as defined in (
3
) to a constant function, we of course get
0
for any
N\in\mathbb{N}
and
t\in[0,T]
, so shifting the linear functional derivative by a constant in Equation (
32
) does not change the representation of the limiting process. A common means of fixing this constant for concreteness is to require that
\langle\mu,\frac{\delta}{\delta m}u(\mu)[\cdot]\rangle=0,\forall\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
(see p.31 of
[
10
]
or Section 2.2 of
[
15
]
. However, due to our choice of topology for the fluctuations process, correcting the constant for the linear functional derivative may break assumptions
A13)
and
A’13)
. We thus interpret these assumptions to mean that there is a choice of constant when defining each of the linear functional derivatives of the functions in question which makes them satisfy the desired properties.

We recall a useful connection between the Lions derivative as defined in
D.1
and the empirical measure.

Proposition D.6
.

For
g\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
which is Fully
C^{2}
in the sense of definition
D.2
, we can define the empirical projection of
g
, as
g^{N}\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{d}
given by

\displaystyle g^{N}(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})\coloneqq g(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\beta_{i}}).

Then
g^{N}
is twice differentiable on
(\mathbb{R}^{d})^{N}
, and for each
\beta_{1},..,\beta_{N}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
(i,j)\in\{1,...,N\}^{2}
,
l\in\{1,...,d\}

(78)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\beta_{i}}g^{N}_{l}(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})=\frac{1}{N}\partial_{\mu}g_{l}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\beta_{i}})[\beta_{i}]

and

(79)

\displaystyle\nabla_{\beta_{i}}\nabla_{\beta_{j}}g^{N}_{l}(\beta_{1},...,\beta_{N})=\frac{1}{N}\partial_{z}\partial_{\mu}g_{l}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\beta_{i}})[\beta_{i}]\mathbbm{1}_{i=j}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\partial^{2}_{\mu}g_{l}(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\beta_{i}})[\beta_{i},\beta_{j}].

Proof.

This follows from Propositions 5.35 and 5.91 of
[
9
]
.

∎

Finally, we provide a Lemma which allows us to couple the interacting particles (
55
) to the IID McKean-Vlasov Equations (
57
) knowing only information about the growth of the linear functional derivatives of the coefficients.

Lemma D.7
.

Suppose
p\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sup_{x,z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}p(x,y,\mu)[z]|+\sup_{x,z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}p(x,y,\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|\leq C(1+|y|^{k})

for some
C>0,k\in\mathbb{N}
independent of
y\in\mathbb{R}
and that
p(x,y,\cdot)\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is Lipschitz continuous in
\mathbb{W}_{2}
for all
x,y\in\mathbb{R}
. Assume
A1)
-
A7)
and
A9)
. Then for
(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon})
as in Equation (
57
) and
\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t}
as in Equation (
58
), we have there exists
C>0
independent of
N
such that for all
t\in[0,T]
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]\leq\frac{C}{N-1}.

Here
\bar{X}^{\epsilon}\overset{d}{=}\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon},\forall i,N\in\mathbb{N}
.

Proof.

This follows using the same conditional expectation argument as on p.26 in
[
15
]
and then following the proof of Lemma 5.10 in the same paper, but where we only require second order expansions rather than 4th. Since the argument and assumptions are slightly different, we present the proof here for completeness.

We first write

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]\leq 2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\qquad+2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\quad\leq C\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\mathbb{W}_{2}(\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})|^{2}\biggr]+2\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

where here
\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t}
denotes
\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t}
with the
i
’th particle removed, i.e.

\displaystyle\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\delta_{\bar{X}^{j,\epsilon}_{t}}.

Recall the formula

(80)

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}^{p}_{p}(\mu^{N}_{x},\mu^{N}_{y})

\displaystyle=\min_{\sigma}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}|x_{i}-\sigma(y)_{i}|^{p}

for
x,y\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
,
\mu^{N}_{x}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}},\mu^{N}_{x}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{i}}
, and where
\sigma\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathbb{R}^{N}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N}
denotes a permutation of the coordinates of a vector in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
(see e.g. Equation 2.8 in
[
25
]
). This suggests that the first term should be bounded due to the bound
C\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t}|^{2}]/N\leq C/N
from Lemma
8.1
.

To see this is indeed true, we take
\mu^{N}_{x},\mu^{N,-i}_{x}
for any
x\in\mathbb{R}^{N}
, where here
\mu^{N,-i}_{x}
is defined in the same way as
\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t}
, and see

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu^{N}_{x},\mu^{N,-i}_{x})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{2}\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)

\displaystyle\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\biggl[\delta_{x_{j}}(dx)+\frac{1}{N-1}\delta_{x_{i}}(dx)\biggr]\delta_{x_{j}}(dy)

We see that indeed
\gamma^{N}
is a coupling between
\mu^{N}_{x},\mu^{N,-i}_{x}
since it is clearly non-negative,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}[N-1][1+\frac{1}{N-1}]=1,

and for
f\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(x)\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\biggl[f(x_{j})+\frac{1}{N-1}f(x_{i})\biggr]=\biggl\{\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}f(x_{j})\biggr\}+\frac{1}{N}f(x_{i})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\mu^{N}_{x}(dy)

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}f(y)\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\biggl[1+\frac{1}{N-1}\biggr]\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}f(x_{j})=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}f(x_{j})=\int_{\mathbb{R}}f(y)\mu^{N,-i}_{x}(dy).

So indeed

\displaystyle\mathbb{W}_{2}^{2}(\mu^{N}_{x},\mu^{N,-i}_{x})

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}|x-y|^{2}\gamma^{N}(dx,dy)=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\left\{|x_{j}-x_{j}|^{2}+\frac{1}{N-1}|x_{j}-x_{i}|^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N(N-1)}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}|x_{j}-x_{i}|^{2}.

Now, applying this to the first term we wish to bound,

\displaystyle C\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\mathbb{W}_{2}(\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N(N-1)}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\bar{X}^{j,\epsilon}_{t}-\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t}\biggr|^{2}\biggr]=\frac{C}{N}\biggl[\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}|^{2}]-\mathbb{E}[\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}]^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N}\mathbb{E}[|\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}|^{2}]\leq\frac{C}{N}

where in the equality we use that the
\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t}
’s are IID, and in the last bound we used Lemma
8.1
.

Now we turn to the second term. We have by independence,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]=

\displaystyle\qquad=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(x,y,\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})-p(x,y,\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]\Bigg|_{(x,y)=(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t})}\biggr].

We will show that for
q\mathrel{\mathop{\mathchar 58\relax}}\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
with two bounded Linear Functional Derivatives, that for
\{\xi_{i}\}_{i\in\mathbb{N}}
IID with
\xi_{i}\sim\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})
, that letting
\xi=(\xi_{1},...,\xi_{N})
and
\mu^{N}_{\xi}
be as above with
\xi
in the place of
x
:

(81)

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[|q(\mu^{N}_{\xi})-q(\mu)|^{2}\biggr]\leq\frac{C}{N}\biggl[\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}+\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|^{2}\biggr].

Applying this to the above equality, we have there is
k\in\mathbb{\mathbb{N}}
such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{\mu}^{\epsilon,N,-i}_{t})-p(\bar{X}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\bar{Y}^{i,\epsilon}_{t},\mathcal{L}(\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}))\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N-1}\mathbb{E}\biggl[1+|\bar{Y}^{\epsilon}_{t}|^{2k}\biggr]\leq\frac{C}{N-1}

by Lemma
7.1
, and the result will have been proved.

We now prove the bound (
81
). By definition of the linear functional derivative, we have:

\displaystyle q(\mu^{N}_{\xi})-q(\mu)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[z](\mu^{N}_{\xi}(dz)-\mu(dz))dr=S_{1}+S_{2}

where

\displaystyle S^{N}_{1}

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z](\mu^{N}_{\xi}(dz)-\mu(dz)),

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[z]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]\biggr](\mu^{N}_{\xi}(dz)-\mu(dz))dr.

For
S_{1}
, we have by independence:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[|S_{1}|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{1}]\right]\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\biggl(\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{1}]\biggr|^{2}\biggr]-\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{1}]\right]^{2}\biggr)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}.

Now we set

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{r}\coloneqq\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]\biggr],

where
\tilde{\xi}
is an independent copy of the
\xi_{i}
’s,
r\in[0,1]
, and the expectation
\tilde{\mathbb{E}}
is taken over the law of
\tilde{\xi}
.

Then we have
S^{N}_{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{1}\phi^{i}_{r}dr
, and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|S^{N}_{2}\biggr|^{2}\biggr]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\phi^{i}_{r}\biggr|^{2}\biggr]dr=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i}_{r}\phi^{j}_{r}\biggr]dr=S^{N}_{2,1}+S^{N}_{2,2}

where

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[|\phi^{i}_{r}|^{2}\biggr]dr,\quad\text{and}\quad S^{N}_{2,2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i}_{r}\phi^{j}_{r}\biggr]dr.

Observing that for all
i\in\mathbb{N},r\in[0,1]
and
\omega\in\Omega
,
|\phi^{i}_{r}(\omega)|^{2}\leq C\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}
, so we have

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,1}\leq\frac{C}{N}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}.

For
S^{N}_{2,2}
, we introduce the measures
\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{\xi}\coloneqq\frac{1}{N-2}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i_{1},i_{2}}^{N}\delta_{\xi_{j}}
for
i_{1},i_{2}\in\{1,...,N\}
, and let

\displaystyle\phi^{i,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{r}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]\biggr].

Then

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{r}\phi^{j}_{r}

\displaystyle=[\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}][\phi^{j}_{r}-\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}]+\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}[\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}]+\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}[\phi^{j}_{r}-\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}]+\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r},

so

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2}

\displaystyle=S^{N}_{2,2,1}+S^{N}_{2,2,2}+S^{N}_{2,2,3}+S^{N}_{2,2,4}

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,1}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}\biggr]dr

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}[\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}]\biggr]dr

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,3}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}[\phi^{j}_{r}-\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}]\biggr]dr

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,4}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\int_{0}^{1}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[[\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}][\phi^{j}_{r}-\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}]\biggr]dr.

For
S^{N}_{2,2,1}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}\phi^{j,-(i,j)}_{r}|\xi_{k},k\neq i,j\biggr]\biggr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j),x}_{r}\phi^{j,-(i,j),x}_{r}\biggr]\Bigg|_{x=\xi}\biggr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j),x}_{r}\biggr]\Bigg|_{x=\xi}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{j,-(i,j),x}_{r}\biggr]\Bigg|_{x=\xi}\biggr]

where

\displaystyle\phi^{i,-(i_{1},i_{2}),x}_{r}

\displaystyle\coloneqq\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{x}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{x}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]\biggr]

and same for
j
. Then

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{i,-(i,j),x}_{r}\biggr]\Bigg|_{x=\xi}

\displaystyle=\biggl\{\mathbb{E}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{x}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\xi_{i}]\biggr]

\displaystyle-\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i_{1},i_{2})}_{x}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]\biggr]\biggr\}\biggl|_{(x=\xi)}=0

since
\xi_{i}\overset{d}{=}\tilde{\xi}
, and same for
\mathbb{E}\biggl[\phi^{j,-(i,j),x}_{r}\biggr]\Bigg|_{x=\xi}
. Thus in fact,
S^{N}_{2,2,1}=0
.

To handle
S_{2,2,2}-S_{2,2,4}
, we need to see how to bound
|\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}|
. We have that

\displaystyle\phi^{i}_{r}-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}

\displaystyle=\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i}]

\displaystyle+\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]-\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(r\mu^{N}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi}]\biggr]

\displaystyle=r\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(rs\mu^{N}_{\xi}+r(1-s)\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\xi_{i},\bar{z}][\mu^{N}_{\xi}(d\bar{z})-\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}(d\bar{z})]ds

\displaystyle+r\tilde{\mathbb{E}}\biggl[\int_{0}^{1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(rs\mu^{N}_{\xi}+r(1-s)\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}+(1-r)\mu)[\tilde{\xi},\bar{z}][\mu^{N}_{\xi}(d\bar{z})-\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{\xi}(d\bar{z})]ds\biggr].

Then using

\displaystyle\mu^{N}_{x}-\mu^{N,-(i,j)}_{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\delta_{x_{k}}-\frac{1}{N-2}\sum_{k=1,k\neq i,j}^{N}\delta_{x_{k}}=\frac{1}{N}\delta_{x_{i}}+\frac{1}{N}\delta_{x_{j}}-\frac{2}{N(N-2)}\sum_{k=1,k\neq i,j}^{N}\delta_{x_{k}}

and that
r\in[0,1]
, we get

\displaystyle|\phi^{i}_{r}(\omega)-\phi^{i,-(i,j)}_{r}(\omega)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{4}{N}\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|

for all
\omega\in\Omega,r\in[0,1],i,j\in\mathbb{N}.
This combined with the fact that
|\phi^{k,-(i,j)}_{r}(\omega)|\leq C\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|,k=i,j
for any
i,j\in\mathbb{N},r\in[0,1],\omega\in\Omega
allows us to see:

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,2}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{N(N-1)}{N^{2}}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|\frac{1}{N}\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N}[\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}+\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|^{2}]

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,3}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{N(N-1)}{N^{2}}\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|\frac{1}{N}\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N}[\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta}{\delta m}q(\mu)[z]|^{2}+\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|^{2}]

\displaystyle S^{N}_{2,2,4}

\displaystyle\leq C\frac{N(N-1)}{N^{2}}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{C}{N^{2}}\sup_{z,\bar{z}\in\mathbb{R},\mu\in\mathcal{P}_{2}(\mathbb{R})}|\frac{\delta^{2}}{\delta m^{2}}q(\mu)[z,\bar{z}]|^{2}.

So the bound (
81
) is proved.
∎

Remark D.8
.

Note we could have polynomial growth in
x
for the Linear Functional Derivatives as well and the result above would still hold, so long as we have sufficient bounded moments for
\bar{X}^{\epsilon}_{t}
. Also, the result is independent of the fact that the particles depend on
\epsilon
, and of the fact that the particles are one-dimensional. See Lemma 5.10 in
[
15
]
and Theorem 2.11 in
[
47
]
for similar results in the higher-dimensional setting.
